17 Years Later, Quentin Tarantino’s ‘Inglourious Basterds’ Reveals the Casting Crisis That Nearly Killed It

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

It's impossible to imagine Quentin Tarantino's World War II epic Inglourious Basterds without the indelible performance of Christoph Waltz as calculating Nazi villain Hans Landa. In fact, the whole project almost fell apart over casting the part — and it almost went to an actor who would go on to have two iconic roles in Tarantino movies. Now, you'll be privy to a dizzying array of behind-the-scenes stories and information in the new book The Making of Quentin Tarantino's Inglourious Basterds. It's on store shelves now, and Collider is proud to exclusively present an excerpt from the book that details how Waltz landed the part that earned him his first Oscar.

The Making of Quentin Tarantino's Inglourious Basterds is written by Jay Glennie, who's penned making-of books for films like The Deer Hunter, Raging Bull, and Tarantino's own Once Upon a Time in Hollywood. It will tell the story of how Tarantino's scrappy war film went from page to screen and became a modern classic in the process. The book will feature never-before-seen concept art; photos of the film's costumes, sets, and props; and an introduction by Brad Pitt. It retails for a suggested price of $75 USD; it is published by Insight Editions and distributed by Simon & Schuster, and is now available.

In our exclusive excerpt, Tarantino is agonizing over casting Landa: he considered the role to be the best part he'd ever written, and he needed the perfect actor to play it. That didn't mean having an English-speaking actor put on a German accent; he wanted a native German speaker who was also capable of fluently speaking the multiple languages Landa speaks in the film. Interestingly, at one point Leonardo DiCaprio was circling the part; he wanted to work with Tarantino, and he spoke some German he'd learned from his German-born mother, but he'd have to wait for Django Unchained. Unable to find a suitable Landa, Tarantino was on the verge of cancelling the entire film when German casting director Simone Bär recommended obscure Austrian character actor Christoph Waltz. Waltz had his doubts about the project, but after nailing his audition, he and Tarantino went on to make movie history.

Four criminal masterminds. One of them was always going to be yours. Here is who comes for you, why they picked you out of everyone available, and what they want from the encounter.

Your nemesis does not want to beat you. He wants to know you completely, and he is patient enough to wait for it. The frightening part is how good the conversation is.

Your nemesis is not after money, territory or revenge. He is after a demonstration, and you are the person he has chosen to prove his point on.

Your nemesis respects you, which sounds flattering until you work out what it means. He chose you because nobody else was worth his time.

Your nemesis has no personal interest in you whatsoever, and that is precisely what makes him unsurvivable. You are not a rival. You are a stop on the way.

Inglourious Basterds centers around a squad of rough-and-tumble Nazi hunters, led by Aldo Raine (Pitt), who are sent behind enemy lines for a campaign of shock and terror. Meanwhile, Nazi-hunting polyglot Hans Landa (Waltz) has been sent to secure a theater in Paris for the premiere of the Nazi propaganda movie Nation's Pride, unaware that the theater is owned by Shosanna Dreyfus (Mélanie Laurent), a Jewish woman whose family was massacred by Landa's men. She has her own plans for the German dignitaries who will attend the premiere, but will those plans survive the arrival of the Basterds?

The Making of Quentin Tarantino's Inglourious Basterds is now available from Insight Editions. Stay tuned to Collider for future updates, and read Collider's exclusive excerpt of the book below.

Quentin was having a full-on panic attack and in danger of falling into a black hole of depression; whichever way around you wished to sell this fucker, it was some dark place. He was in his Berlin hotel room. After a full day of casting and acting alongside every actor Bär had brought into the room, sleep was not forthcoming. Let the rest of Berlin sleep; Quentin was being painfully attacked by night demons.

“I’m the one reading opposite the other actors, so I’m playing all the other characters. It’s when my casting of actors is the best, because I know who these characters are. I know where the jokes are, where the laugh lines are. I know the rhythms. I’m trying to help the actors get the right rhythm. I want them to succeed. I want them to be good. I know how it is supposed to sound, so if they are intelligent, they jump aboard the train because I’m telling them how it is supposed to go. Now, I can get excited if they’re going to throw the train in reverse and try something different that I had never thought of, but there is a rhythm to my stuff for the most part. My mind can be changed. If an actor has a reason and is not doing something different just to do something different for the sake of it, then I can change. But at the end of each audition, I’m absolutely exhausted. I’m wrung out at the end of every day because my scenes aren’t little, they’re big.”

He had cast two main German roles, which should have been a relief, but in his hotel suite that night, sleep wouldn’t come. None of the actors German Casting Director Simone Bär had brought into the room were right for Hans Landa. Quentin was in panic mode. Were his initial fears going to be played out as he prophesied? Was Landa the impossible role?

“The word was out, all right. Hans Landa was one in a million. I was aware enough to know that Landa was the greatest character I had ever written. Everybody knew that after they read the screenplay, and now everybody wanted to play the detective. I heard that a couple of the German actors had an issue doing an American movie and just didn’t come in. They thought what was gonna happen is what often happens, and that is, we were gonna read every German actor, make them jump through hoops and do a poodle show, and then we’re just gonna cast Anthony Hopkins. It happens every single time, an English actor playing a German, and the German actors auditioning have just had to prostitute themselves. But if I say I’m not gonna do that, I’m not gonna do that. I’m not going to cast a Dutch actor, a Swedish actor, an English actor. No. French-, German-, and English-speaking actors are going to play the French-, German-, and English-speaking roles. I wanted the actors to represent the sides that they were on.

“So, I thought that maybe I might have to convince someone that that wasn’t gonna happen, all right? But here was the thing: I was more than a little worried that I had written a character that was unplayable because he has to be both charming and cunning, and then he has to have a facility for languages. He is a linguistic genius. It was not going to work if somebody was going to learn the languages the way an actor was going to do that—it was just not gonna work. Now, the only German actors I’m going to know are if they have been in a big German feature at the time or if they have been in an American movie.

“It was around the time of The Lives of Others, and everybody kept talking about the bald guy, Ulrich Mühe, would be fantastic, but he’s dead. No, that’s the perfect guy, that’s the perfect guy. Yeah, but he’s dead! Let’s fucking move on, all right! If I didn’t cast an actor who was himself a charming linguistic genius, Landa would never be on the screen what he was on the page. I realized that I was going to need a linguistic genius in order to pull this off. There could be no faking it.”

Early on, Leonardo DiCaprio had circled. His mother was German. He could polish his German, for sure he would have to learn French and Italian, but Hans Landa was just the kind of role he wanted to tackle. And it was Quentin Tarantino. DiCaprio had his list of directors he wanted to work with, and you’d best believe that Quentin Tarantino was on that list. Fuck it; he was the top of that list.

A Pitt and DiCaprio combo sure would have been something. The executives at the Weinstein Company and Universal would have been rubbing their hands at that box office. But here was the thing: Quentin never cast just any actor. Now, Leonardo DiCaprio was not just any actor, and no doubt Quentin would have loved to work with him, but Landa had to walk that linguistic tightrope. No, a DiCaprio, Pitt, and Tarantino team-up would have to wait.

In a quiet Berlin hotel suite, he was about to pull the plug because he could not find that high-trapeze artist. As a committee of one, he had come up with the correct decision. If he could not find the most perfect actor to play Landa, the Jew Hunter, the master of detection, the don of language, then he would cancel the movie. Did that hurt? You bet it did. A movie he had worked on for ten years not seeing the light of day, it crushed him.

It was going to be a long dark night. He was seriously despondent, but ultimately it was one of the easiest decisions he had ever made. Inglourious Basterds would not be made without the perfect Landa.

“If I did not find my Hans Landa, then fuck it, I was going to pull the plug on the whole God-darn movie.”

He knew he was going to have to call his producer.

“He called early the following morning. I went along to his room, and he looked awful—he looked like he hadn’t slept,” recalled producer Lawrence Bender.

“He told me that he wanted to pack up and go home. Simone had brought in the best, and among them wasn’t Hans Landa. It was no good. It was a lost cause,” Bender said.

“Okay, hear this,” explained Quentin. “When I tell Lawrence this, I’m not cavalier about it. I’m determined not to make the movie if I do not have the right Landa. So, I’m not cavalier. But from this day forward, I knew I needed to start to emotionally divorce myself from the material. So, I can wield the ax if need be. You know, it is not like I’m burning the script. World War II is not going anywhere. In, say, five years, there will be a new crop of actors, and maybe Landa will be hiding in plain sight amongst them. Maybe one of the guys we’ve seen during this period matures to a more capable Landa five years from now. We’ll see. But as sure as shit World War II ain’t going anywhere.”

“Now, I’m explaining to Lawrence how I cannot go ahead without the Landa, and to give him credit, he didn’t freak out. He didn’t try and talk me out of it. He had been in the room, and there was also kinda the impression that we had seen the best, so, dare I say it, he even agreed with me. This was the best version of Lawrence, the very, very best side of him.”

Like any great producer, Bender listened. Then he gave himself a moment to allow the horrific feeling in the pit of his stomach to momentarily rise. After giving himself that moment, he pushed aside any angst and started to look at the situation from a logical standpoint.

“Here’s the thing: Part of your job as the producer is to fix things, solve problems. A lot of it is creative, but a lot of it is nuts and bolts and making things better,” explained Bender. “After you have dealt with your own disappointment, you go to instinct mode, and my instinct said, ‘Let’s stay.’ So, I said to Quentin, ‘Let’s give ourselves a few days. Here’s what our burn rate is: We’re burning through $100,000 a week, so it is worth you spending another $100,000. Let’s just spend the week looking for Landa. It is worth the risk for you.’”

As down as he was, this made perfect sense to Quentin. Universal would start financing the movie on Friday, so he had just days. If any plug was going to be pulled, it had to happen by Friday.

“Lawrence was 100 percent right. He said, ‘We have got to call the Weinsteins, Universal, Mike, and Carlos, and tell them the situation—and, most importantly, Simone. We have to tell her that for the rest of the week, it is Landa, Landa, Landa, Landa. Nobody else. We ain’t got time to fuck around. We’ve got to find Landa by end of business Thursday night, or we’ve got to shut the film down. It is just that fucking simple. We’ve gotta tell Simone what time it is. It is just that fucking simple. It’s just what we are doing.’ I needed the best of Lawrence, and that was him there and then,” said Quentin.

Casting director Alexandra Montag offered her recollections of the “Finding Hans Landa” casting meeting.

“Simone explained that we had worked to the criteria that Quentin had expressed, and that was that every German-speaking actor speak not only German but also English, French, and Italian authentically. There were very few actors in Germany who had this range, and we agreed that although the actors he had seen were very successful actors, there was something missing. We had seen the tapes.

“But it is also true that not every actor was available on that first day, and he had yet to see an actor Simone believed just right for the role. His name was Christoph Waltz. ‘Please wait for Christoph—Christoph will come,’ she told Quentin. It was her belief that he had all the charm, charisma, and cynicism that fit with Hans Landa.

“I, however, picked up Quentin’s fear as well, and when they had left, I said, ‘Oh shit, shit, shit, what are we going to do?’ But Simone was completely calm and always said, ‘Just wait for Christoph Waltz.’ She was completely confident.”

Making his way to meet with Quentin Tarantino, Christoph Waltz had very little reason to feel optimistic. He had been in his chosen profession since the ’70s, and since leaving the theater world to concentrate on television and film he had become, to a degree, a respected working actor. “Everybody knew who I was; I had played some good parts, of course, I had to do some crap also. But in all those years, Simone Bär had never once cast me in anything. It was odd, but there it was.”

So, not convinced that Bär believed in his talent, Waltz pushed open the door and entered her casting office. He was confident in his own abilities and ready to play the game, like any other actor.

Montag recalled Bär never doubting Waltz’s talent. It was more “because she never found the role for him in the way that this one fit him, the charm, the cynicism, which he could play so well. She knew how good he was. She knew in Landa she had the perfect role for him.”

Waltz, however, was unaware of any such confidence. It was, he felt, another audition he did not need to get excited about.

“We had had it happen many times before: an international filmmaker coming in to cast their film. When Spielberg came to cast Schindler’s List, everyone was in hysterical convulsion: ‘Spielberg, Spielberg, Spielberg.’ Yes, it is all exciting, but frankly speaking, what is in it for me, personally? When you are offered the role of a German soldier in the ghetto who shouts, ‘Get out, get out, get out’ for $500, why would I want to do that?

“So, that was my mindset. I’m an extremely skeptical character, and when they gave me the whole screenplay, it was very heavy, with its title page written in Quentin Tarantino’s own hand, and from there you can get a feeling of the person who wrote it. I said to my agent in Germany, ‘Okay, what role do they want me to do?’ ‘Well, you should read for that Landa character,’ I was told.

“‘Okay, I’ll read Landa.’ Now, I was convinced that I was only reading this big part of Landa because they wanted me for a part that didn’t have enough text to actually show anything. It was just text material.”

Ushered into the kitchen, Waltz took a seat and waited his turn to enter the audition room.

This was the landscape of the time. If a big American film production came to Germany or Austria, local actors would be passed over, so television was where one made a living. It was, Waltz said, how it was.

“We, the natives, would be left with the shitty small parts, and usually the plum parts went to English actors who put in blue contact lenses, dyed their hair blond, and put on a phony accent which no one has. I was used to that. It was out of the question to think it would be any other way. So, when Quentin came, I was under the impression that what always happened would happen this time. They’re in town for the tax credit. They’ll do their search for local actors, hold up their hands that nobody suitable can be found, and an English-speaking actor will play the part. I had it pretty much sussed out how it worked. Not that I’m correct in everything, but experience told me that, you know, I did not need to get excited.”

He had been assured that this time was different. Waltz decided that, if nothing else, he would enjoy reading with Quentin Tarantino, and that would be that.

“Nice to meet with you, Mr. Tarantino. Nice to meet you, Mr. Bender.”

Sitting opposite the American director and producer, the first thing to strike the actor was the director asking him if he would mind reading the opening scene. “Would I mind?” he thought. “Are you kidding?”

“But of course,” said Waltz, nodding in agreement.

Waltz’s Colonel Hans Landa introduced himself to the French farm owner, Perrier LaPadite, on this occasion played by Quentin Tarantino.

“First of all, from the get-go, Lawrence and I kept looking over at each other, and our smiles just became bigger and bigger and bigger,” enthused Quentin.

“We were literally kicking each other under the table in excitement,” agreed Bender.

Waltz’s complete absorption into the scene was nearly broken when he thought to himself, “Why is Quentin sucking his thumb?” Instantly, he realized the banality of such questioning. Perrier LaPadite smokes a pipe. The man in front of him, the writer of the weighty screenplay, was pretending to smoke one.

The room was devoid of any noticeable cameras. Instead, the great Quentin Tarantino was encouraging him to play, like a kid. He had never felt so safe, so relaxed, in an audition room. Nobody was looking at him probingly, questioning how he had the temerity to even be in the room. His natural pessimism waited for the curveball that was sure to come.

Quentin, on the other hand, never witnessed anything other than devotion to what he had written from the actor who sat in front of him. “It was amazing. It wasn’t as though none of the other actors had done the scene well. Some had done it well, but they didn’t own the role, especially that scene. The scene was an incredibly dangerous mountain that they managed to scale and survive, but they didn’t own the mountain—they just got to the top. Waltz owned the mountain.

“By the time we finished reading the scene, we knew that he had the role. He had saved the movie; he absolutely saved the film. So it is over with, and Lawrence and I are absolutely losing our fucking minds. We are just so effusive, and Christoph is just being very casual about it. He is looking at us as if we are just a couple of gregarious Americans. I pick up that he is thinking we have said this to everybody, and we are blowing a little sunshine up his ass. So, I make it plain: ‘No, no, you understand this character. You’re fantastic, you’re great.’

“To which Christoph replied, ‘Well, thank you very much. That is very nice to hear. However, I’m of the belief—and sometimes I run afoul of my colleagues in this regard—that an actor is only as capable as the role that he is playing. If you want an actor to be great, you need to give him great material. So, when you are effusively telling me how magnificent I am, in fact, you are just applauding your own material. Because, yes, I’m quite good playing this role, but that is because it is a damned good role, but ultimately, you are just complimenting yourself.’

“To which I reply, ‘Well, thank you very much. But be that as it may, you cannot deny that when it comes to this role, you have an inner compass.’

“He thinks about it for a second and responds by saying, ‘Okay, possibly I have this compass of which you speak. Nevertheless’—he points at the script—‘the North Pole is the North Pole.’

“Ha! ‘Well,’ I say, ‘you have just done the scene in English, and you obviously speak German?’

“‘Of course I speak German—I’m from Austria.’

“‘Can you do a little bit of it in German for us?’

“He picks up the script, and away he goes. It is perfect. ‘There are also parts written in French. Could you give us a little of it in French?’

“Picking up the screenplay written in English, he translates it into French, and he is doing it perfect. So, I ask him, ‘There is also a little in Italian—do you speak Italian?’

“Of course, he fucking nailed it! We found our guy!”

Quentin called his friend Tom Tykwer [director of Run Lola Run and who had been tasked with translating Quentin’s script into German]

“I found him, I found him, I found him! I found Landa!”

A bomb went off in Tykwer’s head. “Of course you did! Why did I not think of Christoph?”

Regis le Bris admits these 5 Sunderland players could miss the Newcastle United derby

Sunderland boss Regis le Bris has revealed that at least five Sunderland players could miss the derby match against Newcastle United at St James’ Park.

That game is in 17 days time on Sunday 6 December with a 2pm kick-off.

Regis le Bris announcing on Thursday that all five of these Sunderland players will miss the Premier League away game at Villa Park on Saturday.

The quintet that definitely miss the weekend’s Aston Villa away game are Brian Brobbey, Dan Ballard, Granit Xhaka, Romaine Mundle and Habib Diarra.

Two of these five Sunderland players will for sure miss the Newcastle United game, Regis le Bris saying today: “Daniel Ballard and Romaine Mundle will be back training in mid-December.”

The way the Sunderland manager was speaking, it appears very unlikely that Brian Brobbey or Granit Xhaka will be available for the derby.

On Brian Brobbey, Regis le Bris saying today: “It is a big injury for Brian, which we will have to assess weekly. It is a new injury for us but it is too early to know the timeline. He is sad and disappointed because he has worked so hard. He was a monster against Chelsea, so I think it is important for us and him to reset. I hope that he will use this period to get better.”

As for Granit Xhaka, Regis le Bris stating he won’t be back until after Brian Brobbey: “For Granit it is a bit longer, it is a calf injury.”

The only one of the five Sunderland players who appears to have any kind of realistic chance of featuring in the Newcastle United match is Habib Diarra.

With Regis le Bris explaining: “For Habib Diarra it is going well and we will see in the next two weeks or so.”

The SpaceX pattern suggests a possible relief rally, but Bitcoin's previous recovery was followed by another downside move.

Bitcoin’s latest price decline is drawing comparisons with its pre-SpaceX IPO correction, raising speculation that history could repeat as Anthropic makes its public-market debut. BTC has dropped toward $62,000 after a sharp sell-off, closely mirroring the weakness seen before SpaceX’s IPO earlier this year.

With Anthropic’s debut adding a fresh market catalyst, traders are now watching whether Bitcoin can stage another relief rally, or if the current sell-off has further room to run.

Bitcoin has declined nearly 28% from its September peak near $87,400, with selling pressure intensifying ahead of Anthropic’s November 19 debut. The cryptocurrency briefly dropped toward $62,000 before recovering above $63,000.

Bitcoin is repeating the exact dip it made before the biggest IPO in history.If history repeats, we could see another leg up after a few weeks of sideways price action. pic.twitter.com/vQpjUAxhyn

The decline closely resembles Bitcoin’s movement before the SpaceX IPO in June. During that period, BTC dropped from around $83,000 toward $59,000, marking a sharp correction ahead of the company’s public listing.

The similarities have fueled speculation that investors may be reducing exposure to liquid assets ahead of major IPOs, potentially creating temporary selling pressure across crypto markets. However, the broader decline also reflects weakening risk appetite, ETF outflows, leveraged liquidations and macroeconomic uncertainty.

Bitcoin’s recovery following the SpaceX IPO initially strengthened the historical comparison. After SpaceX went public in June, BTC staged a short-term rebound, climbing from around $63,000 toward $66,000 over the following days. However, the recovery did not mark the market bottom. Bitcoin subsequently fell again, reaching a lower level later in June before beginning a broader recovery that eventually carried it toward its September high.

This suggests the IPO-related pattern may involve a temporary relief rally rather than an immediate trend reversal. If the same sequence repeats, Bitcoin could recover in the short term before facing renewed selling pressure.

Anthropic’s IPO has become a major market event, with the company pricing shares at $83, raising approximately $81.3 billion through its public offering. The broader transaction is expected to exceed SpaceX’s record fundraising when additional private placements are included.

The listing has also coincided with a rebound in technology stocks and Nasdaq futures, improving risk appetite across financial markets. Bitcoin recovered toward $64,000 as equity futures strengthened, suggesting that sentiment surrounding the IPO could influence short-term crypto price action.

However, the relationship remains uncertain. Bitcoin’s recent decline has also been driven by broader market deleveraging, institutional flows and macroeconomic concerns, meaning the IPO alone may not determine its direction.

Bitcoin’s immediate recovery remains fragile after the sharp decline toward $62,000. The $62,000 zone is the critical support level. Holding above this area could allow BTC to stabilize and attempt a recovery toward $65,000–$67,500. A sustained move above $67,500 would strengthen the short-term bullish outlook and potentially open the path toward $70,000.

However, a breakdown below $62,000 could expose Bitcoin to further downside toward $59,000, followed by the $57,800 region, which represents a key previous low.

Bitcoin’s pre-IPO decline has created a compelling comparison with the SpaceX listing, and Anthropic’s debut could provide a short-term catalyst for a recovery.

Yet the previous cycle also showed that an initial rally can fade before the market establishes a durable bottom. Traders will therefore watch whether BTC can hold $62,000 and regain $67,500 in the coming sessions. If history repeats, Bitcoin could see a relief rally, but the next move may not be as straightforward as bulls expect.

Dig review: Amy Poehler and Parks and Recs Mike Schur have done it again

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The first time Amy Poehler and Mike Schur teamed up for a series about Poehler rolling up her sleeves and getting to work in a massive dirt pit, they gave us Parks and Recreation, a charming workplace sitcom about the power of friendship and doing what you love. The second time Amy Poehler and Mike Schur teamed up for a series about Poehler rolling up her sleeves and getting to work in a massive dirt pit, they gave us Peacock's new series Dig, which is — you guessed it! — a charming workplace sitcom about the power of friendship and doing what you love.

Story-wise, the two shows are miles apart. Literally. The former takes place in the decidedly unglamorous town of Pawnee, Indiana, while the latter, based on Kate Myers' novel Excavations, transports us to a sun-bleached archaeological dig in Greece. Yet despite the flashy location and a new mystery twist, Dig retains the core formula that made Parks and Recreation so irresistible: investing deeply in a lovable new crew of unlikely friends.

Kicking off that group is Elise (Poehler), the lead archaeologist on a long-running dig in southern Greece. She's qualified and passionate, yet has a hard time connecting with the undergraduates (or UGs) participating in the dig. Because of her more guarded exterior, she's consistently second fiddle to longtime dig leader Neville (Hugh Laurie). Perpetually on the brink of retirement, Neville seems unable to cede control of the dig to Elise, both because of his love for archaeology and the ego boost his role gives him. He's the Lucy to Elise's Charlie Brown, constantly yanking the football away at the last second.

So, when Elise discovers a perfectly preserved statue of Hera at an older dig site, the last thing she wants to do is tell Neville and give him an opportunity to claim any shred of credit. However, hiding any cultural artifacts from Neville and the Grecian government at large is illegal, so Elise must assemble a crack team to help her cover up her covert dig of the Hera statue, whom they nickname "Pretty Woman." First up is Dylan (Geraldine Viswanathan), an always flaky wild card who has unfinished business at the dig. Joining her are Clare (Antonia Thomas), a prim and proper conservator born with a silver spoon in her mouth, and Patty (Fina Strazza), a sheltered farm girl with a deep passion for Greek mythology.

Like with most sitcoms, the characters begin as the broadest versions of themselves before revealing their deeper nuances. At first, Elise is prickly to a fault, allowing Poehler to sidestep any preconceived comparisons you might want to draw to Leslie Knope. Elsewhere, Dylan is the hottest of hot messes, Clare is a haughty queen bee, and Patty is an out-of-her-depth klutz. They border on parodies instead of full-bodied characters, making Dig's earlier episodes a bit lukewarm.

But as Dig's title suggests, this is a show that won't be satisfied by sticking at surface level. As the show delves into what makes its characters tick, it becomes a heartwarming tale of found family, not unlike many other beloved Schur shows. Suddenly, character traits that felt one-note to start are revealed to be defense mechanisms or misconceptions, adding entirely new dimensions to Dig's core four.

Dig works best when that core works together in earnest, which is another reason why the show's early entries feel like slower burns: They're doing the groundwork to loop every woman into the Pretty Woman dig. Once they're all on the same page, Dig gets off and running — even more so once Pretty Woman mysteriously vanishes, leaving Elise and her motley crew to do some boots-on-the-ground detective work. Thus begins a madcap caper involving stakeouts, undercover identities, and the black market.

Along the way, Elise, Dylan, Clare, and Patty are allowed to be wonderfully messy and vulnerable, all while staying refreshingly supportive of one another. For example, a love triangle burbles up between Clare, Dylan, and Clare's fiancé/Dylan's ex-boyfriend Gary (Jon Pointing), yet the storyline never devolves into cat fights or possessiveness. Instead, Dig focuses more on what these relationships mean to Clare and Dylan beyond romance. For Clare, her engagement is a sign of stability and true adulthood, whereas Dylan is looking for closure, something she notoriously can never get.

Dig also dabbles in intergenerational conflict, highlighting the differences between each of its leads, all of whom are at very different stages in their lives. Again, Dig doesn't take a hackneyed "Gen Z versus millennials versus Gen X" route. It's more interested in showing the different wants that motivate us. Patty yearns for belonging and friendship, while Elise wants to cement her legacy and make sure she's done something big enough to call her own, no Neville required.

Once Dig's ensemble truly comes together, they mesh wonderfully. Poehler brings a weary exasperation to Elise that slowly gives way to warm leadership, a transformation that's a joy to witness. Thomas and Viswanathan are perfect foils, the former a picture of poise, the latter completely uninhibited. Watching them feed off each other offers up a perfect comedic contrast. Finally, there's Strazza, whose endearingly goofy Patty becomes the beating heart of the show. Laurie, too, is a joy, blending Neville's smarminess with a genial charm that makes him infuriatingly tough to hate.

In focusing on archaeology, Dig emphasizes the importance of excavating ancient objects and interrogating the stories of those who once used them. In many ways, Dig is doing the same thing with its characters: peeling back their layers to get at their core truths. Only instead of using trowels and brushes, Dig uses the power of the workplace sitcom. It's a power Schur and his collaborators know well, and the result is a show that will leave you feeling like you've just uncovered something great in a dig of your own — with far less dirt on your face and clothes, that is.

All episodes of Dig premiere Nov. 23 on Peacock. Recommended deals for you HBO Max — top pick for premium content Disney+ — best for families Peacock — low-cost choice for live sport

‘The Traitors: New Blood’ Renewed for Second Season at NBC

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The renewal comes just before the show wraps its initial run.

NBC will head back to Scotland for a second season of The Traitors: New Blood.

The network has renewed the show, a civilian spin on Peacock’s hit competition show The Traitors. The pickup comes just before New Blood’s first season finale, which will play out over two hours Thursday night.

Alan Cumming, who hosts both U.S. versions of The Traitors, will continue his role in season two of New Bloods and the forthcoming fifth season of the celebrity iteration of the series, which premieres in January on Peacock. Cumming has won three consecutive Emmys for best host for a reality or competition show. Before streaming, The Traitors: New Blood is averaging about 2.9 million viewers over seven days. Romano states Milan could be an option for Balde with Mendes links: “Discuss a strategy”

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

According to Fabrizio Romano, AC Milan could be involved in the market for Alejandro Balde once again this winter.

A central defender would be the main target for most Milan fans ahead of the winter mercato, but the possibility of signing another wing-back wouldn’t be turned down. In fact, with the left-side still proving to be a puzzling area, someone may need to be recruited.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

In the past, Balde has been linked and there have been several other suggestions. However, the Spaniard may be the most accessible mid-way through the season and it would seem that the Rossoneri are considering him again.

Speaking on his YouTube channel, Romano looked at the left-back’s future and didn’t deny that the Diavolo could be involved for another mercato, as MilanNews relayed.

“In the last few hours, Barcelona’s sporting director Deco spoke about Alejandro Balde and said that they are not planning on Balde’s exit because we are only in mid-November; there is a month and a half to go until January.

“Barça play every two or three days and a player like Balde can clearly still be useful between now and the transfer market. It’s true that Barça has left-back cover at the moment, but Barça doesn’t want to put a player like Balde on the market publicly already in mid-November.

“The reality behind the scenes is different: Balde remains a player who could leave in January, because Jorge Mendes, the player’s agent, was in Barcelona this week to speak with the player and discuss a strategy.

“Balde could therefore leave, but we must be realistic because there are significant figures at stake, given that he earns more than €5 million net and in the summer Barcelona were asking for €50 million for him.

“We’ll see if the formula can change in January, but on the one hand, Deco says so, on the other, there’s the reality: if Balde doesn’t play as well as he hasn’t been playing between now and January, the idea of ​​Balde leaving could become concrete.

“We told you that he’s currently being offered to Roma, but the Giallorossi haven’t really picked up steam yet. They’ve also offered him to Milan; the player was initially proposed last summer, but without a transfer, nothing happened.

“We’ll see what happens with Alejandro Balde, but he’s a name for the international or possibly Italian market that we need to keep there.”

Considering Sankhoun Diawara is now being considered as a left-back, the situation is certainly not ideal. So, Milan could certainly do worse than signing Balde.

Mortgage Rates Surge Again as Stubborn Inflation Ends Brief Reprieve

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Mortgage rates are rising again after four consecutive weeks of declines, as hotter-than-anticipated inflation data sets the stage for another interest rate hike, putting upward pressure on Treasury yields.

The average rate on 30-year fixed home loans jumped to 7.28% for the week ending Nov. 19, up 17 basis points from 7.11% recorded the week before, according to Freddie Mac. For perspective, rates averaged 6.26% one year ago.

"While prospective borrowers often shop around for a home, they may not always think to do the same for their mortgage, as comparing multiple rate quotes can potentially save them thousands over a loan's lifetime," says Sam Khater, Freddie Mac's chief economist.

The October Consumer Price Index (CPI) report came in above expectations last week on both the headline and core measures, all but guaranteeing that the Federal Reserve will raise its benchmark rate to curb inflation at the next Federal Open Market Committee (FOMC) meeting on Dec. 8.

The looming hike, combined with the persistence of the war in Iran, pushed the 10-year Treasury yield back toward 5% this week, putting mortgage rates on an upward trajectory once again.

"The debate has shifted from whether the Fed hikes in December to how aggressively it moves," says Realtor.com® senior economist Anthony Smith. "That is a tough backdrop for mortgage rates heading into what is already a seasonally slow stretch for the housing market."

Markets are currently pricing a 93% chance of a 25-basis-point increase next month, according to the CME FedWatch tool.

Meanwhile, pending home sales fell to a record low in October, and existing home sales hit their lowest level since 2010, as the surge in mortgage rates through mid-October took its toll on buyers and sellers.

Earlier this week, the National Association of Home Builders (NAHB) reported builder confidence falling to a new 14-year low, even as permit activity dropped to its lowest level since the early days of the pandemic, signaling that the supply pipeline will face continued pressure in the months ahead.

For buyers who remain active on the market, current conditions offer some meaningful advantages, including less competition from other shoppers, inventory climbing 8.6% year over year, and sellers increasingly willing to negotiate on price.

This week, the median listing price fell 2.4% from a year ago to $405,000, matching its steepest decline since early October, according to the latest Realtor.com weekly housing market update.

"The challenge is that until inflation cools enough to put mortgage rates on a durable downward path, affordability will remain the dominant constraint on the market," says Smith.

Your credit score plays a role when you apply for a mortgage. A credit score will determine whether you qualify for a mortgage and the interest rate you'll receive. The higher the credit score, the lower the interest rate you'll qualify for.

The credit score you need will vary depending on the type of loan. A score of 620 is a "fair" rating. However, people applying for a Federal Housing Administration loan might be able to get approved with a credit score of 500, which is considered a low score.

Homebuyers with credit scores of 740 or higher are typically considered to be in very good standing and can usually qualify for better rates, which can reduce monthly payments.

Snejana Farberov is a reporter at Realtor.com covering the U.S. housing market and the latest domestic real estate trends. She has worked as a general assignment journalist in New York City and Long Island for 16 years, writing for New York Post, Daily Mail, and News 12. Snejana earned bachelor's degrees in journalism and Italian from St. John's University, followed by a master’s degree from Columbia University School of Journalism.

Brian Brobbey's value to plummet after latest, Sunderland will regret not cashing in

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Brian Brobbey has struggled to retain his fitness as Sunderland battle against Premier League relegation this season.

His impact in the final third couldn't have been anymore evident during the Black Cats' 1-0 win over Chelsea before the international break.

The centre-forward could only last 67 minutes, though, and it appears that he's picked up yet another problem after sustaining a hamstring injury before.

Brobbey was one of the key reasons for Sunderland's success last season, helping Regis Le Bris' side seventh-place finish in the Premier League.

His efforts didn't go unnoticed, with several clubs around Europe, including Chelsea and Manchester United, targeting the 24-year-old's signature.

Football Insider understood that even Nottingham Forest were interested, at least before they splashed £50million on Liam Delap.

The problem now, however, is that Brobbey has picked up another injury, and Le Bris says it is far too early to add a return timeline to the situation.

While the manager is confident that it's not a "big injury," it is another blow that the Black Cats didn't need, one that seriously needed avoiding.

With so many clubs interested during the summer, there undoubtedly would have been a chance to cash in, but the club didn't take it.

Now, the Black Cats are left wondering who they might have brought onboard using that money, while Brobbey requires more medical treatment.

Still, Football Insider understands that Sunderland will only consider selling if they are relegated from the Premier League.

Right now, Brobbey is valued at just over £41m, which is already lower than the potential price tag that the Black Cats had slapped on him.

That number could drop by at least £5m in the coming weeks, especially if Le Bris' not-so-bad news turns into something more serious.

If the worst is to happen and Sunderland finish in the Premier League relegation zone, there is no way that they'd be able to fetch a fee anywhere that high.

Maybe resisting the summer interest wasn't the best idea, but foresight is a wonderful tool, and not one that anyone has.

Anthropic hits Nasdaq exchanges at $111, 33% above IPO price

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Shares of Anthropic are now trading on the Nasdaq exchange, as the company successfully executes its initial public offering. In the early minutes of availability, shares were trading at $111.50, a 33% premium over the $83 IPO price.

Set on Wednesday, the IPO price was already higher than the projected range set by the company, which anticipated a price between $70 and $78 per share. All told, the offering will raise over $81 billion, directed to a combination of employees and the company’s own coffers.

The IPO funding, together with existing fundraising and new private offerings timed to coincide with the company’s public market debut, will leave Anthropic with an expected $220 billion in cash reserves. As one analyst told TechCrunch, “Anthropic’s going to have more cash on hand than any company has ever had.”

The share price continues to fluctuate, but the opening price would put the company’s market cap over $2 trillion, making it more valuable than Meta, Tesla, or Walmart. Only five companies boast a higher valuation by market cap: Apple, Amazon, Alphabet, Nvidia, and Microsoft.

In a speech before the opening bell, Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei emphasized the company’s commitment to safety. “Today puts the company in a stronger financial position, and in doing so, only strengthens our resolve to do the right thing,” Amodei said. “If we can show it’s possible to make the right choices on safety and still build an incredibly successful company, others in our industry will be inspired to do the same. We call this a race to the top.”

A Trick To Buy Accor Hotel Stays At 40% Off — And Black Friday May Make It Even Better

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Loyalty Lobby flagged Accor’s pre-Black Friday subscription sale. Combined with a weakened Brazilian currency this creates an opportunity.

Accor is much bigger than its presence in the United States would have you think. The French hotel company finished September with 5,857 hotels and 885,219 rooms. They operate in more than 110 countries, with a huge presence in Europe and extensive coverage in Asia and Australia.

Their brands include Fairmont, Raffles, Sofitel and MGallery at the luxury end, along with Swissôtel, Pullman and Mövenpick. Then there are Novotel and Mercure, and the budget ibis brands. U.S. options include Fairmont Austin and Raffles Boston.

I took advantage of the Bilt status match I wrote about this summer, which offered up to Platinum.

Accor points are worth 2 euro cents apiece toward eligible hotel stays. The familiar redemption is 2,000 points for €40. Using November 18 exchange rates, that’s about $43.87, or 2.19 U.S. cents per point.

There’s no hotel award chart. An expensive room requires more points. But you can apply points to paid rates without hunting for a limited supply of standard award rooms, and a lower room rate means fewer points needed also.

Reward points normally expire after 12 months without eligible earning activity. A qualifying points credit extends the whole balance, even if you earn just one point. Partner earning can keep the account alive without another hotel stay. An active ALL Signature subscription also protects Reward points from expiration.

Here’s the calculation using the published monthly prices multiplied by 12, less 20%, and November 18 exchange rates. Dollar amounts are rounded.

Your card’s conversion rate will also differ slightly, and be sure to use a card without foreign transaction fees.

The Explorer plan is the better pure points purchase. You’re buying hotel credit for about 29.8% below its redemption value, compared with 23.8% for Absolute. Absolute buys more points and more status credit, but each redeemable point costs more.

Unfortunately, annual payment doesn’t make all the points available immediately. Absolute deposits 5,000 points a month, plus another 5,000 every fourth month, for 75,000 over 12 months. Explorer delivers 3,000 monthly plus 3,000 every fourth month, totaling 45,000. You can’t sign up and immediately spend the full year’s balance on a hotel booking.

You can stack this with ALL Accor+ Explorer, a separate paid membership offering 15% off eligible rates at participating hotels worldwide, plus benefits concentrated in Asia Pacific. Accor uses “Explorer” for both a Signature subscription tier and a different membership. Apparently someone thought that was a good idea.

Buying discounted points and then spending them against an eligible discounted room rate is where this gets better. Combining a 15% room discount with points purchased 29.8% below face value produces about 40.3% off the underlying room price, before the separate membership cost and the rewards you’d otherwise earn.

Signature’s monthly Status points are separate from its redeemable Reward points. Absolute supplies 1,000 Status points a month, or 12,000 over a full year, versus the 14,000 needed for Platinum. Those points count in the calendar year they’re credited and reset at year end.

There’s also an escape valve if your hotel plans change: Accor points transfer to airlines.

Many other partners offer a worse ratio. British Airways, for instance, takes 4,000 Accor points for just 2,000 Avios. Use Iberia for Avios rather than accepting half as many through British Airways.

At the Explorer subscription price above, you’re acquiring points for about 1.54 cents each. That’s also the acquisition cost of a mile through a 1:1 transfer. But once you have the points, moving them to an airline means giving up about 2.19 cents each in hotel credit.

The Cuckoo rice cookers you have seen all over Tik Tok are on sale for as low as $28 during Amazon’s Black Friday sale

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 19, 2026 12:56 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn more ›

Maybe it’s just me, but I have been seeing tons of cooking videos on social media featuring the Cuckoo rice cooker. I typically use an Instant Pot for cooking rice and it’s…fine. But, these prices on the Cuckoo machines make me want to jump into the dedicated rice cooker crowd. You can’t put a price on fluffy rice. (You can, actually, but that price is lower here during Black Friday deals).

For more of what’s on sale, our Amazon Black Friday deals hub has picks across categories.

The Cuckoo 8-Cup Micom Rice Cooker is $84.99, which is 29% off. Cuckoo sizes it for eight cups of uncooked rice, enough to yield about 16 cups cooked, so it suits a household that goes through rice most nights or anyone who batch cooks for the week. The company’s fuzzy logic system adjusts heat and timing as the pot cooks, and the nonstick inner pot and auto clean mode keep cleanup short.

Induction heat without the pressure-cooker price

Induction is the big step up from a basic heating plate. Cuckoo says the system in the Cuckoo 8-Cup Induction Heating Rice Cooker heats the whole inner pot instead of only the bottom, and at $191.98 (40% off) it costs far less than the brand’s pressure models. It has 12 menu modes, including GABA rice, scorched rice, porridge and yogurt. Cuckoo also lists a turbo mode that cooks a single serving in about 20 minutes, plus voice guidance in English, Korean and Chinese.

Pressure when you want it, open cooking when you don’t

Twin pressure is the feature to know here. The Cuckoo 6-Cup Twin Pressure Rice Cooker lets you pick pressure cooking for stickier, chewier rice or non-pressure cooking for fluffier grains, and Cuckoo gives it 16 menu options to cover both. It holds six cups uncooked (12 cooked) and talks you through settings in three languages. It’s $232.55 during the sale, or 32% off.

Seven temperature presets for tea drinkers

The deepest discount in the sale isn’t a rice cooker. The Cuckoo 1.7L Electric Glass Kettle with Tea Infuser drops to $47.90, which is 47% off. Cuckoo builds it with seven temperature presets from 104 to 212 degrees Fahrenheit, a detachable infuser for loose-leaf tea and a keep-warm function that the company says runs up to two hours after boiling. The 1,500-watt base heats a full 1.7 liters.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/healthcare-purchasers-must-weigh-hospital-quality-alongside-their-prices" hreflang="en">Healthcare purchasers must weigh hospital quality alongside their prices, Leapfrog says</a>

📅 2026-11-19  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
Healthcare purchasers must weigh hospital quality alongside their prices, Leapfrog says" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Patient safety watchdog The Leapfrog Group released its latest collection of hospital safety grades hand-in-hand with calls for policymakers and stakeholders to consider, or directly tie, patient safety reporting with price transparency.

The twice-yearly release brought top marks for 900 out of a total 2,366 graded hospitals, and the lowest “F” score for just three. There were 774 hospitals that earned a “B” grade, 655 a “C” grade, and 35 a “D” grade.

No grades were assigned to another 447 hospitals, a decision the group made after a judge’s ruling in a lawsuit involving the scores it would assign to facilities that did not voluntarily submit their quality data to Leapfrog’s survey (Leapfrog appealed that decision in September). Leapfrog’s decision not to score these has generally shifted its letter grade distribution upward, and the organization has said it is reviewing a potential methodology change for upcoming gradings to incorporate non-submitting hospitals.

Still, “a record number of hospitals are choosing to publicly report their safety and quality performance, and we applaud them for holding themselves accountable to their patients,” Leapfrog President and CEO Leah Binder said in a statement.

At the state level, Connecticut maintained its claim on the largest portion of hospitals earning an “A” grade (64.3% of its total), followed by Utah, New Jersey and South Carolina. Leapfrog also called out Rhode Island and Maine for jumping up the rankings—from eighteenth to seventh for the former, and from seventeenth to eighth for the latter. No hospitals were given an “A” grade in five states: Iowa, North Dakota, South Dakota, Vermont and Wyoming.

A hospital’s assigned grade is calculated by reviewing recent data on up to 22 patient safety measures, including a 10-part Medicare composite of reported patient safety and adverse events. Safety scores for specific hospitals can be searched on the group’s website.

Leapfrog and Binder, in a news release outlining its latest scores, made the case that its grades and similar independent measures warrant a seat at the table in a transparency conversation primarily focused on hospital costs.

“Progress on price transparency is important, but there’s no good price for bad care,” Binder said. “We are calling on the Trump Administration and Congress to ensure price transparency is integrated with patient safety and quality transparency.”

The group backed its calls by pointing to a recent analysis (PDF) conducted by the Purchaser Business Group on Health (PBGH) that reviewed employer claims, federal price transparency data, and quality and safety measures including Leapfrog’s. It “found little to no correlation between negotiated prices and standardized quality measures,” as hospitals that received high safety grades often charged cheaper prices than those in the same market with lower grades.

Leapfrog and Elizabeth Mitchell, president and CEO of PBGH, said the analysis—currently being expanded for practical usage by healthcare purchasers—is an example of how a more wholistic view of prices and care delivery should be considered when weighing a hospital.

“Employers have an appropriate duty to pay a fair price for high-quality care, and they can’t meet it by looking at price alone,” Mitchell said in the release from Leapfrog. “When we put the data together, some of the most expensive hospitals were not the safest. Our members want to buy high-quality, safe care that is fairly priced. That shouldn’t be too much to ask.”

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: Should I walk away from my career and pursue a custodian position?

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

I think there's a bit of warm glow nostalgia going on about these blue collar jobs.

If you work in an Amazon warehouse you are *monitored*. They count the number of steps you take & "counsel" you if you are too slow. Same for all the other jobs there.

You are, in fact, a humanized robot. We know this from various attempts to unionise in USA & overseas (UK here).

Blue collar jobs that pay decent hourly rates & have health insurance benefits etc are probably more fiercely fought over than white collar jobs.

Physically your ability to do repetitive physical labour at 50 is not the same as it was at 18 or 25. There's a reason so many opiate prescriptions were written. Chronic occupational pain is a thing.

And you are bossed around by somebody who is 24 & on the way "up". They are not all people you want to work for.

If one has a particular craft or skill like electrician or carpenter that's different. A good plumber can make more than an office worker. Although physically it begins to tell - arthritic knees & elbows for example. (I would imagine in USA a good plumber can make 80-100k pa or more even, but would have some costs of self employment?)

I don't envy the Eastern European lady who empties our office garbage & resets the coffee machine. She's hard working as heck, and probably in her late 50s/ early 60s.

OP should:

- collect unemployment benefits for as long as able
- keep looking in their own field but a wider range of employers eg 3 levels of govt. I would avoid Higher Education given demographic & other threats, except for good public universities & well endowed private ones.
- consider a wider travel radius
- if it looks like benefits run out then think again

If the blue collar route looks more likely, talk widely to the folks who do these jobs. And understand the physical demands etc.

Dave Challinor needs to raid Stockport County in January to sign 12-goal striker for Sheffield Wednesday: Opinion

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Sheffield Wednesday will get the chance to bring in some new players when the January transfer window opens up for business. SWFC’s new boss Dave Challinor will be looking to put his own stamp on his newly inherited squad this winter with some reinforcements.

The Owls’ manager knows Stockport County striker Kyle Wootton well from their time at Edgeley Park together. He should now look to lure the experienced attacker away from the North West to Hillsborough to bolster his squad in South Yorkshire.

Wootton, 30, is a proven goal scorer at League One level now and he would give Challinor’s side more firepower up top if they managed to snap him up. He is out of contract at the end of the season so the Hatters risk losing him for free in June 2027 if they don’t cash in on him in January, assuming he doesn’t pen an extension anytime soon.

Sheffield Wednesday have risen back into the play-offs following their 3-0 win over Plymouth Argyle at home last time out and they are in the top six with Oxford United, Luton Town and the Pilgrims. They face Barnsley away on Friday night at Oakwell.

Sheffield Wednesday could do with more of a focal point in attack. Standing at 6ft 3inc, Wootton would be ideal for SWFC and would give them something different to pick from.

Challinor signed him for Stockport County back in 2022 and he helped them rise from the National League to the third tier. He has made 270 appearances for his current club in all competitions to date and has chipped in with 70 goals, 12 of which have come this season, and 30 assists.

If the Owls secured his services, he would give them more competition and depth in his position. In addition, he could battle it out with the likes of Jamal Lowe, Mason Burstow and Davis Keillor-Dunn for a place in Challinor’s starting XI.

Prior to joining the Hatters, he found the net on 59 occasions in 137 outings for Notts County. He has also had spells in the past with the likes of Scunthorpe United, Lincoln City and Stevenage, among others.

Sheffield Wednesday were linked with Portsmouth’s Colby Bishop in the summer but weren’t able to get a deal done in the end. Wootton fits a similar mould and would be a great alternative target now for SWFC.

He knows Challinor, is proven and could be available for a cut-price amid his uncertain Stockport County contract situation. Therefore, it seems a no-brainer that they lodge a bid for him.

The Owls will be in confident mood after their victory against Plymouth Argyle. They will be eager for more of the same against Barnsley under the lights.

Lewis Hamilton: &quot;You can't be the only one pushing for change&quot; in F1

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Lewis Hamilton says he had hoped others in Formula 1 would follow his lead in speaking out on issues around the world, but admitted that "so far that's not the case".

The seven-time world champion has used his platform for causes including diversity in motorsport and, more recently, the humanitarian crisis in Gaza.

Asked by the media in Las Vegas ahead of this weekend's race whether more people in the sport be using their platforms to speak out, the Briton said everyone was entitled to their own view, but called on the championship to do more as a whole.

"I just try to lead with intention and hope that, I've always thought, kind of, if you can be that pebble that creates a ripple and encourages other people to maybe follow in your footsteps but so far that's not the case. And honestly, I don't know what everyone else stands for, but that's not really my concern," Hamilton said.

"My concern is just trying to, as I said, try to do the best I can and stand for the right things, regardless of what people say. I just follow my heart, and I'm really proud of the work that I've done with my foundation."

Hamilton said F1 was moving "in a positive direction" with projects such as the diversity charter signed by the FIA, F1 and the teams, but is adamant that the responsibility of making a change could not fall on just one person.

"There's more we can do. It's a huge business, valued at over 20 billion. You can't be the only one pushing for change and positive things like that. You need to all work together. So I'm hoping more and more people will come along on the journey," he said.

Hamilton also spoke about his visit to Egypt last week, two days after the Sao Paulo Grand Prix, when he was shown the aid operation at the Rafah crossing into Gaza.

"I really wanted to go and see it for myself. Worked with an organisation that's doing some amazing work, but it was, you know, heartbreaking and very, very heavy. I met children that had been shot, and burned, and disfigured," he said.

"But it was amazing to see that there are some good people on the ground that are doing some really amazing work to try and help these youngsters recover, give them education."

The Ferrari ace who visited Jordan with the British Red Cross last December, said it was important to maintain coverage on these subjects.

"I think that no child in the world should ever be responsible for, or be affected by a government's decision. And it's really just about the human side of things. We really stand for humanity. And while I have this platform, I will continue to do that," Hamilton ended.

CHN Energy: Two Decades in Indonesia, Empowering Growth, Bridging Friendship

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

BEIJING, Nov. 19, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- China and Indonesia are close neighbors across the sea and valued partners with a shared future, bound by a friendship that stretches back centuries. Twenty years ago, China Energy Investment Corporation (CHN Energy) first entered Indonesia's energy market. Ever since, in this archipelagic nation of countless islands set amid vast blue waters, CHN Energy has continued to deliver a stable and reliable supply of clean energy, helping strengthen the friendship between China and Indonesia.

For 20 years, China Energy Investment Corporation (CHN Energy) has been putting its commitment into practice in Indonesia, growing alongside the country's energy sector and local communities.

CHN Energy aims at building a world-class energy conglomerate with global reach. Through its Indonesian operations, CHN Energy has worked to provide reliable electricity while building a strong local presence through technology, talent development and community engagement.

The documentary Two Decades in Indonesia: From Small Lights to a Blazing Torch looks back on this journey through the experiences of the people who have helped shape it. At the PT. GHEMM Indonesia, employees recall the challenges of the early years, including surveying the plant site and advancing construction amid muddy swamps and sweltering heat, drying and utilizing Indonesia's high-moisture lignite, Chinese and Indonesian teams worked side by side to overcome the challenges, optimize production processes, and develop new technological solutions. Over time, cooperation has grown beyond technical collaboration. Indonesian employees have developed their skills through hands-on experience and close mentorship from Chinese colleagues. What began as a teacher-and-apprentice relationship has, in many cases, become a bond of friendship and family. Cultural exchange, mutual respect for local customs and shared everyday experiences have helped bring colleagues closer together.

The Group's commitment also extends beyond the power plants. In Indonesia, its teams have worked with local communities to create employment opportunities, improve infrastructure and support environmental initiatives. By the end of 2025, a total of 43,000 mangrove trees had been planted to help restore and protect coastal ecosystems.

Looking ahead, Indonesia's "Golden Indonesia 2045" vision calls for energy security alongside the continued expansion of renewable energy and improvements to energy infrastructure, paving the way for a cleaner, lower-carbon and more sustainable electricity system. CHN Energy looks forward to working hand in hand with Indonesia to help shape a greener energy future.

Opinion: Julian Ward will sign €30m South American wonderkid for Liverpool

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Julian Ward has been thrown into the deep end as Liverpool's sporting director.

Over the last couple of windows, the Reds' transfer regime has been handled by Richard Hughes.

The Englishman managed to spend well over £600 million during his time as the Reds' sporting director and, after managing to make the team worse despite this huge amount of spending, he chose to pack his bags and leave for pastures new.

This left Liverpool looking for a new person to take the reins with Fenway Sports Group eventually landing on Julian Ward as their preferred option.

Since then, the rumour mill has been in overdrive and now that we're edging closer to the transfer window opening back up, some of these links are going to have to turn concrete and actually get completed.

Liverpool want to sign Arthur Dias from Athletico Paranaense

One of the main transfer strategies that Liverpool have had over the last couple of years is signing young players for the future.

They've always had one of the best academies in the world but now, they're clearly looking for more ways to set themselves up.

So far, most of their transfers have worked out and, while the jury is still out for some of them such as Milos Kerkez, they're all still incredibly young and have a lot of their careers left to go.

But, as much as Liverpool have youngsters among their ranks, they also have some veterans who many need replacing in the future.

As mentioned, many different players have been linked with Liverpool but now, thanks to an update from FussballEuropa, Brazilian centre-back Arthur Dias has been linked.

"Liverpool FC has its eyes everywhere – including on the Brazilian market. The Reds' scouts are keeping a close eye on Arthur Dias. The race for the talented center-back is already on," they wrote.

"Athletico Paranaense could have made a significant sum from Arthur Dias last summer. Tottenham Hotspur showed concrete interest and reportedly submitted an offer of just over €20 million.

"However, this was too low for Paranaense, and consequently, no deal was reached.

"Dias has even earned two caps for the Brazilian national team. This automatically makes the rising star a target for other clubs. According to information from Fussballeuropa.com, Premier League side Liverpool are keeping a close eye on Dias.

"The Reds are regularly monitoring the young defender and keeping a close eye on his development.

"As Fussballeuropa.com has also learned, Paranaense are demanding a transfer fee of at least €30 million. The 2001 Brazilian champions will reportedly not even enter negotiations for anything below this magic number."

Dias is probably a player that hardly any Liverpool fans have heard of.

But, the fact that he's 21 years old and has already been given two international caps for Brazil proves how good he actually is.

The centre-back is one of the most highly-rated prospects in the Brazilian Serie A and, the fact that he's available for around £25 million is mad.

Obviously, the step up from the Brazilian Serie A to the Premier League will be a big one but, since Jeremy Jacquet managed it, there's no reason why Dias can't too.

Many transfers are likely to be made by Liverpool in 2027 but, if Dias isn't one of them, they'll be leaving themselves rather red-faced.

Here's hoping that Ward and Andoni Iraola can align their transfer values and bring Dias in.

When is Nations League draw? Start time, confirmed pot numbers and TV channel for quarter-finals

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The 2026-27 Nations League draw for the quarter-finals takes place this week, as the business end of the competition comes into focus.

England are among the eight teams to have qualified for the knockout phase, with Thomas Tuchel’s side enjoying a near perfect group phase.

Their only defeat came against reigning world champions Spain on matchday one in September, though the Three Lions exacted revenge on Sunday night as they held on after being reduced to 10-men to seal a famous victory.

As a result, England topped Group A3 at Spain’s expense due to a superior goal difference and that means they are likely to avoid another European heavyweight in the quarter-finals.

Elsewhere, France sealed top spot in Group A1 with a 2-1 win over Turkey, while Belgium thumped Italy 4-1 to finish as runners-up.

In A2, Germany topped the table with a 5-0 thrashing of the Netherlands, who slipped to second on goal difference after such a swing.

Portugal’s position was Group A4 was confirmed after their win over Wales on Tuesday night, though it is Norway joining them in the knockout phases following a remarkable comeback win against Denmark.

The Nations League draw for the 2026/27 quarter-finals will take place on Friday, November 20, following the conclusion of the group phase fixtures.

As well as the quarter-final draw, the promotion/relegation play-offs draw will also be conducted.

Coverage of the draw is due to start at 11am GMT, with the draw itself expected to begin shortly afterwards.

The Nations League draw will take place in Nyon, Switzerland.

TV channel: In the UK, the draw will be televised live UEFA TV.

Live stream: You can also stream the draw for free online via the UEFA YouTube page.

Live blog: You can follow the draw as it happens on the day through Standard Sport’s live blog.

The four group winners will face the four runners-up in a two-legged Nations League quarter-final.

The group winners will all be seeded and will have home advantage in their respective second leg ties. Teams from the same group cannot be paired together in the knockout stage, therefore England and Spain - for example - will be kept apart.

The four group winners will be seeded in Pot 1, with the four runners-up will be unseeded in Pot 2.

Pot 1: France, England, Germany, Portugal

Pot 2: Belgium, Spain, Netherlands, Norway

*Portugal are guaranteed a Nations League quarter-final spot, though can still finish first or second in Group A4.

The Nations League quarter-finals, as well as the League A/B and League B/C promotion/relegation play-offs, will be played in March 2027.
