How Premier League's big six keep getting richer

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Ten games into the Premier League season, four of the top six spots are occupied by 'big six' clubs.

Only second-placed Brighton, a club with a model which must be the envy of many of their rivals, and Premier League ever-presents Everton, have muscled their way in.

In pure financial terms, it should come as no surprise.

The six biggest clubs - Manchester United, Liverpool, Manchester City, Arsenal, Tottenham and Chelsea - now generate hundreds of millions of pounds more than the division's other teams.

BBC Sport breaks down how the finances of the big six have exploded, examines new trends and the impact on the transfer market, and asks how much it really matters.

Companies put their money where supporters are prepared to spend it. It is a simple fact of business in every country, a reality of football's food chain.

In England, Liverpool, Arsenal and Manchester United traditionally dominated viewing figures, column inches, shirt sales and sponsorship.

Over the past 20 years they have been joined by Chelsea, Manchester City and Tottenham.

This group of clubs now generate so much money it is hard to imagine how they can fail in the long term - even factoring in short-term performance issues.

We will use Everton, the only club not in the big six to be in the Premier League since its inception in 1992.

This tells a tale of how even one of the league's founding members can be left trailing well behind.

In 2009-10, the gap in revenues between the big-six average and Everton was Â£120m. In 2024-25, that gap had grown to Â£442m.

In large part it is the boom in international TV rights.

Kieran Maguire, professor of football finance at the University of Liverpool, told BBC Sport that it has attracted companies prepared to pay large sums to be associated with the Premier League's top clubs.

"The big football brand clubs have global fanbases and that makes them very attractive to global commercial partners, especially for sponsorship and front-of-shirt deals," Maguire said.

"You combine that with the fact that the Premier League is broadcast to 190 different countries around the world, and especially the international audiences - that allows the clubs to negotiate very good prices."

Only 10% of Everton's revenue comes from commercial deals, while for the big six it is 45%.

It means Everton, and other clubs in the league, are heavily reliant on broadcast income. It makes up 66% of their revenue.

For Everton at least, this should change following the move to Hill Dickinson Stadium.

But that is unlikely to be enough to stop the gap increasing.

It has taken a few years, but Aston Villa and Newcastle have begun to increase their revenue.

This is largely thanks to the financial rewards of Champions League football.

But even Uefa has created a system which entrenches the position of the biggest clubs.

When the European Super League plan was torpedoed, Uefa created a new financial deal called the value pillar.

This reserves 35% of all Champions League revenues, or Â£713m, for the historically most successful clubs.

At the end of the league phase last season City are estimated to have earned Â£37.9m from the value pillar, Liverpool Â£35.5m and Chelsea Â£33.3m. Newcastle received only Â£13.6m.

The good news for Villa is their coefficient has improved greatly, aided by last season's Europa League win.

Their value pillar is projected to be worth Â£25m this season. But that will still be less than the big six clubs in the competition.

The cards are stacked against an ambitious club - not just domestically, but in Europe too.

Take a look at the financial trend of the big six and one thing becomes noticeable - Chelsea are beginning to fall behind.

In the past two seasons, and for the first time in recent history, the Blues recorded the lowest revenues of the big six.

Not just by a small amount. They were Â£74m behind Tottenham, and Â£200m below Liverpool and City.

Stamford Bridge has a capacity of only 40,000, with no easy scope for expansion. The rest of the big six have grounds which hold at least 60,000.

Chelsea generated an average spend per fan of Â£80 in 2024-25, the second highest in the league after Arsenal.

Yet with 20,000 fewer tickets sold than their big six rivals, Chelsea's total matchday revenues are dwarfed.

"Matchday spend has become increasingly important," Maguire said. "There has been a flatlining of broadcast revenues, especially since 2017.

"As a consequence, clubs have had to look at alternative methods of increasing revenues, especially with costs both on the pitch and off pitch rising so fast."

Matchday revenue has effectively stalled for Chelsea, rising only Â£16m over the past 10 years. United's has grown by Â£69m and Spurs, thanks to their new ground, by Â£85m.

"Chelsea are far more reliant upon player sales," Maguire said. "There is a growing divide because the global appeal is very much aligned with three or four clubs.

"Arsenal have re-established themselves with their progress in the Champions League. Manchester United are a brand in their own right. And Liverpool have expanded Anfield from 44,000 to 61,000, with many of those extra seats having gone to commercial."

Chelsea's successful player trading model has so far limited the impact.

But there are signs that, financially at least, the big six could become the big five.

You might recall that Villa and Newcastle were not fans of the old profit and sustainability rules (PSR).

PSR forced both clubs to sell some of their best players to balance the books.

That has changed this season with squad-cost ratio (SCR).

There is a key difference with SCR, in that it allows a club to spend the money they generate on players. PSR limited losses so was more restrictive.

"This does make it a little easier for the likes of Villa and Newcastle to spend money," Maguire said. "But it is still heavily weighted in favour of the big six.

"SCR is different to PSR rather than better. For some people, change is advantageous. For others, it isn't."

The big six generate hundreds of millions of pounds more than the other clubs. So they can spend hundreds of millions of pounds more on their squads.

This makes the big six even more powerful in the transfer market.

"Look at the clubs who voted against SCR - Brighton, Brentford, Bournemouth, Crystal Palace, Fulham and Leeds," Maguire said.

"It's very noticeable that those clubs have traditionally been very good at player sales, while they have tended to have lower matchday and commercial revenues."

Most of the big six - the outlier being Chelsea - are not remotely close to the Premier League's 85% SCR limit.

If you give clubs more money to spend on transfers, the likelihood is they will do just that. And the numbers back that up.

Premier League clubs spent Â£3.5bn - not only a highest total for a summer window, but it almost smashed the record for a whole season, the Â£3.53bn set in 2025-26.

The annual accounting cost of previous transfers, or amortisation, has been rising year on year.

"Over the course of the last decade, transfer amortisation has more than doubled and close to tripled," Maguire said.

"Which means that on average, clubs in the Premier League are now spending, or now have a cost which goes into their SCR calculations of just under Â£100m."

Uefa fears that SCR will make matters worse, and have an inflationary impact across Europe.

In the summer 13 Premier League clubs broke their transfer record.

Brentford, Brighton, Palace, Leeds and Nottingham Forest all spent more than Â£40m on a player - unthinkable until recently.

So we have learned that the big six have more money than ever, and the gap is so huge it is impossible for any other club to compete with them, financially at least.

And this is an important point: other than Villa and Newcastle, the other clubs are not trying to compete with the big six.

Clubs like Brentford and Brighton have shown that smart models are just as effective as fat wallets.

They sign the potential stars of the future, nurture them and then sell them to the big six.

Football's ecosystem recycles the big-six money through the transfer market.

But it is a precarious business for these other clubs.

"The smaller clubs have to get it right on a more regular basis than the bigger clubs," Maguire said.

"The big six can afford to make signings which are Â£60m and Â£70m, who turn out to be relative flops, and still have those players on high wages for several years."

And this is perhaps the key point about the vast finances of the big six.

The commercial power of the big six does not guarantee success. After all, United sit in the relegation zone.

Yet Mauricio Pochettino, their new manager, may well get the chance to revamp the squad despite the club spending Â£945m on players in the past five years.

The richest clubs can try to spend their way out of trouble. Again and again.

But the rest of the league are only a couple of bad transfer windows from catastrophe.

And that is the real gap between the big six and the rest.

How Uefa’s internal politics could have cost it the chance to oust Infantino – and what that says about modern football

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Infantino now looks likely to hold onto power in March’s elections, while the complex nature of football politics has resulted in Uefa increasingly mirroring Fifa

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Uefa’s statements may have claimed “unanimous” support for Philippe Diallo as a Fifa presidential candidate from their members, but there isn’t quite such a united front in private. Some national associations, and other stakeholders, believe no one should have been entered so as to delegitimise the entire process.

Instead, Diallo, the president of the French federation, may be facing up to a landslide defeat to Gianni Infantino.“He doesn’t stand a chance,” in the words of one figure who ostensibly backed him.

There is one big “if”. That may admittedly change for Diallo or respected Japanese candidate Kozo Tashima if a big calculation from Uefa president Aleksander Ceferin pays off. There is at least some possibility that their legal action, or four months of political pressure from the Democrats winning the House and scrutinising all of Donald Trump’s connections, may create problems for Infantino. Or, at least, voter doubt. As one insider said, “we’re counting on this - it could be a game-changer.”

As it is, the incumbent Fifa president has weathered an awful lot. In any normal politics, the World Cup privatisation disgrace would have been enough to finish someone. In football, the patronage system has actually ensured Infantino has gathered strength as time has passed. This is why it’s always been so difficult to oust an incumbent. They still oversee how the Fifa money is distributed, no matter the noise.

One effect of the summer’s crisis, in fact, has actually been to increase that money. That’s enough for most voters. There is even the estimation that between five and 20 European countries will end up voting for Infantino, out of the same dissatisfaction with Ceferin that sees them politically pressured into stances like "unanimously" backing Diallo. “The Fifa money stands above everything,” one executive says.

That’s precisely why some sources feel there should be no candidate at all. If Infantino actually beats someone, he will be newly emboldened by a new mandate.

Either way, Uefa’s response wasn’t supposed to look like this. Arsene Wenger was long expected to be their “superstar” candidate, and sources say Emmanuel Macron was even involved in trying to convince him. The wait went on for weeks. Some federation heads have been stunned that Wenger didn’t stand. They felt he might want to end his own association with Infantino by running against him.

At 77, though, it didn’t suit. Wenger probably wouldn’t want a heavy defeat to Infantino in his legacy, as has been the case with other prospective candidates. Zlatan Ibrahimovic’s name was even floated. Amid the prospect of other candidates Uefa wouldn’t want, they felt they had to pick someone.

So, they are left with one repeatedly described as “a nobody”. Others insist Diallo is “a proper politician”, that can prise votes from Francophone Africa… but it does sound like rationalisation after the fact. As one high-profile football executive bluntly put of the whole Uefa strategy, “it’s a disaster”. And it may leave yet more fall-out for the game.

As it is, Fifa are unlikely to come close to any proper reform. And that will ultimately be because Uefa has never come close to reform: Ceferin himself has too much power.

This is something that was often obscured in the understandable frenzy around the Fifa Forward Enterprise crisis. Uefa were insisting it was actually about Fifa as a body, but that should have been extended to them. The same debates about Infantino exceeding term limits can be applied to Ceferin. The same debates about an undemocratic patronage system exist in Uefa.

Uefa and Fifa are a mirror of each other, right up to questions about how the Champions League is now being run under a new commercial body with European Football Clubs (EFC). And for all that Uefa insist this isn’t “personal”, the Slovenian’s acrimonious relationship with Infantino is well known, to the point he has led this resistance. Ceferin has already been known to use unfavourable comparisons to Infantino’s idea to - somewhat disrespectfully - disparage people in meetings. There, he’s also seen as an increasingly abrasive and even “rude” figure.

Sources talk of how the intense scrutiny on Infantino has actually emboldened him further. That comes in a situation where Ceferin is increasingly surrounded by “yes men”, since anyone who goes against him is cut out.

Different voices might have offered alternative counsel in this fight. Instead, so much has been anchored to Ceferin’s own approach, when many would say long-term strategy isn’t where he is strongest. Others would add that feel for the game isn’t where he’s strongest either. One experienced source describes him as “the weakest link in the power chain”.

And there is now the possibility that all of this will leave Ceferin blindsided from two different angles. On one side, Infantino may end up winning comfortably. On the other, there’s a risk to what Uefa are. A third key figure in football right now is that of Nasser Al Khelaifi, Paris Saint-Germain president and chair of EFC. The Qatari was even cast as a challenger to Infantino himself, but The Independent has previously reported how the Emir of Qatar did not want this. The state may end up with a lot of influence anyway.

These pages have frequently reported how Al Khelaifi has a very close relationship with Ceferin, that is almost reflected in how the Champions League is now run. A memorandum of understanding between the two bodies has this week been the subject of a legal complaint from the Union of European Clubs (UEC) over the formation of a monopoly, that has entrenched the power of the big clubs. How the European Commission responds could have considerable repercussions.

This was all as commissioner Glenn Micallef himself set out a new model of European sport, which is supposed to encourage the idea of football clubs from anywhere being able to compete. It’s been a big week in football politics.

The friendship with Al Khelaifi is meanwhile seen as going deeper than that with former Juventus president Andrea Agnelli, who made Ceferin godfather to one of his children. They don’t speak any more, after Ceferin was somehow blinded to the Super League that Agnelli spearheaded.

And what is the current talk in the club game? Al Khelaifi himself announced that “now is the time to create World Football Clubs”, extending the EFC, in the same week that most were preoccupied with the Manchester City scandal. The EFC would dispute this but that is widely seen as a first step to a similar memorandum with Infantino’s Fifa, and eventually the creation of global competitions that could feasibly be challengers to the Champions League.

Except, this time, they wouldn’t be breakaways. They’d be fully institutionalised, because of these same politics over a longer period. What’s more, Uefa might end up indirectly funding a body that ends up affecting its power. Al Khelaifi, whose ultimate responsibility is to the Emir, is viewed as “playing all sides” in a way that is consistent with Qatari state strategy. As La Liga president Javier Tebas said at the UEC’s general assembly in Brussels this week, “he doesn’t trick me”.

Again, various figures involved would absolutely dispute these interpretations. Others insist it’s all consistent with the lack of strategy that has left Uefa with this situation. They had four months to prepare but time has instead been an ally of Infantino.

One view is that they should have forced a Fifa Council meeting when Infantino was most vulnerable. Others maintain that change at Fifa is only ever coming through the courts. Uefa, ironically, may end up facing a similar situation. Then, again, it’s all the same problem.

Arsenal are keeping a close eye on Club Brugge winger Carlos Forbs as Mikel Arteta considers adding more depth and pace to his attacking options.

The 22-year-old has rebuilt his reputation impressively in Belgium following difficult spells with Ajax and Wolves, and his performances this season have now attracted interest from several Premier League clubs.

Arsenal’s admiration comes at an interesting time, with Forbs’ value rising quickly following his breakthrough with Portugal.

According to Fichajes, Arsenal are monitoring Forbs as a potential addition to their wide attacking options.

The Portugal international has produced five goals and eight assists in 15 appearances across all competitions this season, while his latest international outing further increased the attention around him.

Forbs came off the bench to score Portugal’s winner in a 3-2 Nations League victory over Wales on November 17, wearing the No.7 shirt previously associated with Cristiano Ronaldo.

His market value is now estimated at around €23-24million, although Club Brugge could reportedly demand closer to €30m if his form continues.

Forbs remains under contract until 2029, meaning the Belgian champions are in no rush to negotiate.

There is plenty to like about this potential move from Arsenal’s perspective.

Forbs possesses genuine acceleration and one-versus-one ability, qualities that could give Arteta another way of stretching deep defensive blocks. His ability to play from either flank would also reduce Arsenal’s reliance on Bukayo Saka and provide greater rotation across a demanding season.

However, Arsenal are far from alone in watching him.

A CaughtOffside report revealed that Manchester United and Tottenham have also monitored Forbs, highlighting how quickly his performances have attracted Premier League attention.

That competition could push his price higher.

Arsenal should still be cautious. Forbs struggled during his previous loan spell at Wolves, so there remains a question over whether his improved Belgian form would translate immediately to the Premier League.

At around £20m-£25m, though, the risk looks manageable.

Forbs would not need to arrive as an automatic starter. He could instead provide pace, depth and long-term upside behind Arsenal’s established attackers.

If his development continues, moving early could prove much cheaper than waiting another year.

Takaichi gets chilly reception in China on first Japan leader visit since 2019

📅 2026-11-19  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi received a subdued welcome on her first trip to China as premier, signaling Beijing's limited appetite for repairing ties strained by a simmering row over Taiwan.

At gatherings such as this week's Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit in Shenzhen, protocol details — including who greets a visiting leader at the airport and where they are positioned in official photographs — often convey political messages.

Takaichi was met on the tarmac Wednesday by a retired Chinese diplomat, rather than a serving official, and Japan's ambassador to China, Kenji Kanasugi.

The low-key reception contrasted sharply with the warmer welcomes given to other visiting leaders, including South Korean President Lee Jae Myung, while U.S. President Donald Trump was greeted personally by Chinese President Xi Jinping.

In the leaders' group photo, Takaichi was placed in the back row, off to the side and several places from Xi, who stood alongside Trump and Russian President Vladimir Putin.

"Beijing sought to downgrade her official status to that of a head of government rather than a head of state," said Jeremy Chan, senior analyst at Eurasia Group.

During Thursday morning's greeting line, Takaichi spent several minutes trying to talk to the Chinese leader without an interpreter present, while most leaders exchanged handshakes and kept walking.

"It has become increasingly difficult for her to engage in formal or even informal conversations with Xi," said Kei Koga, an associate professor at Nanyang Technological University in Singapore, who sees Takaichi's intention as to still "project the image that she could still communicate with him despite the ongoing tensions."

At a press briefing Thursday afternoon, Takaichi said Japan's position on Taiwan remained unchanged as stated in the 1972 Japan-China Communique, adding that her leadership has been communicating with Chinese counterparts on rare earth export controls.

Takaichi's subdued reception signaled Beijing's lack of enthusiasm to repair ties with Tokyo, analysts said, potentially prolonging China's curbs on critical mineral exports that have gripped Japanese industries for the past year.

"China's frosty reception of Takaichi shows quite clearly that Beijing is not yet ready to forgive her over her remarks about Taiwan," Chan said, expecting the bilateral relationship to remain strained until Takaichi leaves office.

That could leave Japan grappling with continued restrictions on rare earth supplies from China next year, said Tianchen Xu, senior economist at the Economist Intelligence Unit.

Relations deteriorated sharply after Takaichi said in November 2025 that a Taiwan Strait crisis could constitute a situation threatening Japan's survival and warrant a self-defense response.

While the substance broadly aligned with Japan's existing security policy, previous prime ministers had generally avoided stating it so explicitly in a Taiwan scenario.

China reacted angrily and demanded that Takaichi retract the remarks. She has not done so, while stating that the door to dialogue with Beijing remains open, with minimal high-level contact over the past year.

China has since tightened restrictions affecting Japan, including controls on dual-use and rare earth exports with potential military uses. It also discouraged its citizens from traveling to Japan and halted Japanese seafood imports.

Takaichi's visit marked the first trip to China by a Japanese prime minister since Shinzo Abe traveled to Chengdu in December 2019 for a trilateral summit with Chinese and South Korean leaders.

No formal Takaichi-Xi meeting has been publicly scheduled during her visit. Meanwhile, Xi is expected to join Trump and Putin for a historic trilateral gathering on Thursday, alongside a bilateral meeting with Trump.

Takaichi needs to show a "reflection of what she said about Taiwan a year ago," said Huiyao Wang, founder of the Beijing-based Center for China and Globalization, which is often seen as close to Beijing's thinking.

Wang said Tokyo should show greater flexibility, pointing to Trump's comments that the U.S. would avoid getting involved in the Taiwan issue. He argued that Japan holding firm on the issue would not help relations with Beijing.

Such reflection, he said, "would really help to clarify some of those misunderstandings between the two countries."

— CNBC's Evelyn Cheng contributed to this report.

MTG Star Trek Card Featuring Patrick Stewart Sells At $26K

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Magic: The Gathering isn't exactly a stranger when it comes to ridiculously expensive cards, and while resellers aren't quite as rabid as some other TCGs, even somewhat less popular sets like Star Trek are still reaching hefty prices on the second-hand market. While the price might not compete with Lord of the Rings' over $2M One Ring card, getting one of MTG's new serialized cards will still cost you quite a bit.

While the seven hand-signed card variants introduced in MTG's Star Trek Collector's Boosters were already going to be a massive hit due to their limited availability, one of them has already broken the $20,000 mark, albeit for a bit of a morbid reason.

As shared by moombahh on Reddit, while most autographed Star Trek cards go for around $5-10K USD, a recent eBay listing for none other than Captain Jean-Luc Picard reportedly sold for a whopping $26K. While it's entirely possible the buyer sent a lower offer, it might even end up on the cheaper side of the spectrum if the other eBay listings are anything to go by.

With more recent listings having shot up all the way to $38K and an even more massive $45K, the price could wind up being well above the last sold price, and it's not hard to see why either.

For the first time ever, you can pack signed MTG cards, with seven to collect in total, but the first two revealed are of Captains Kirk and Janeway.

Dwarfing the Secret Lair card prices, the main reason players have been pricing their cards so high is that, since signing the cards, Jean-Luc Picard's actor, Patrick Stewart, recently passed away, leaving the serialized cards among the last items signed by the legendary actor.

While fans have pointed out that it's more than a little depressing that others are trying to capitalize on the late actor's passing by selling the cards for absurd prices, it's unlikely that sellers will be slowing down anytime soon, considering how limited their availability will continue to be.

DP World Tour Offers Star Invites To LIV Defectors

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The professional golf world faces a new reality as the DP World Tour moves to fill its fields with high-profile names from the collapsing LIV Golf circuit. Nine players who left their original tours to join the Saudi-backed league have been offered a path back through sponsor invitations, a move that prioritizes star power over traditional meritocratic rankings. This shift comes as LIV Golf winds down through bankruptcy proceedings, leaving many players in limbo while tour officials scramble to stabilize the game's ecosystem.

According to Rediff, European Tour chief executive Guy Kinnings acknowledged the rapid shift in the sport, stating, "There has been significant change in the landscape of men's professional golf over the past 12 months." The tour is now offering these invitations without the hefty appearance fees that characterized the LIV era, aiming to inject excitement into lower-ranked events. This strategy targets specific stops that need a boost in star power, ensuring fields remain competitive even if depleted of top-tier members. According to Ronan MacNamara from Irish Golfer Magazine, up to 34 individuals previously associated with LIV are set to retain DP World Tour status in the coming year, a figure appearing inflated due to the former league's hostile maneuvers against the tour.

The tour has secured the returns of major stars like Jon Rahm, Tyrrell Hatton, and Bryson DeChambeau, which officials view as a positive step for the DP World Tour. CODE Sports reports that Cameron Smith has also been offered a career lifeline following the collapse of LIV Golf, though the terms come with specific conditions. Dustin Johnson, Bubba Watson, Cameron Smith, and Joaquin Niemann are among those receiving a "specific number" of tournament invitations to beef up events where they play. These players, who previously defected with a seemingly endless supply of Saudi cash, now face a different reality as that funding disappears. The arrangement allows these high-profile names to compete without taking spots from lower-ranked players who earned their way through the system. Kinnings emphasized the pragmatic nature of this approach, noting that the tour has always kept the best interests of its entire membership at the forefront of its mind.

Longtime supporters of the European tour have raised concerns about the implications of this policy. The tour previously sent 10 players to the PGA Tour annually, yet now it risks banning PGA Tour players with greater profiles than those it is losing. The idea behind the sponsor invitations is to place these stars in European tour stops that need help, giving those tournaments a boost without taking away spots from fields that were already depleted. This creates a unique situation where players who never contributed to the tour are now being welcomed back with open arms. The move promises to be exciting for European golf fans and head honchos at the Tour, but it leaves rank-and-file members questioning the fairness of the system.

As the DP World Tour integrates these former LIV players, the relationship with the PGA Tour remains a critical factor. With the PGA Tour's partnership with Europe ending next year, talks continue to alter and prolong the agreement. PGA Tour CEO Brian Rolapp confirmed on Wednesday evening that discussions are productive, stating, "both entities have a mutual desire to extend and modify our partnership." The modification will likely include how many PGA Tour cards are offered to leading players in Europe, but it is most certain to be fewer than before. This reduction in card availability adds another layer of complexity to the integration of these high-profile returnees.

Kinnings believes the upcoming season will be highly competitive despite these changes. He noted that with the return of major champions and the inclusion of former LIV stars, "Our 2027 season is set to be one of the most keenly contested campaigns in our 55-year history." The inclusion of players like Rahm and DeChambeau adds significant weight to the tour's schedule. However, the financial stability of these players remains uncertain as the LIV league dissolves.

The next major development will be the finalization of the 2027 season schedule and the specific allocation of cards for European players. The PGA Tour and DP World Tour must resolve their partnership terms before the season begins. The outcome of these negotiations will determine how many European players can access the PGA Tour and how the remaining spots are distributed.

Senate Budget chair called Social Security a 'scam' — said shut it down

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Wisconsin Republican Ron Johnson made the remarks Wednesday, on the day of a Senate Budget Committee hearing on the program

Sen. Ron Johnson, the Wisconsin Republican who chairs the Senate Budget Committee, said on Wednesday that Social Security is a "scam" that should be "shut down," according to MarketWatch.

Johnson made the comments during a Fox Business segment, according to MarketWatch. "It's grotesque and at this point, there really is no way to fix Social Security," he said. "It is a Ponzi scheme. You can't fix it. You just shut it down and hope you can recover some of your money."

The Senate Budget Committee scheduled a hearing titled "Social Security: The Reality" for 11 a.m. ET on Wednesday in room SD-608. The committee lists Johnson as presiding and Sen. Jeff Merkley, an Oregon Democrat, as giving a member statement.

The committee lists three witnesses: Veronique de Rugy of the Mercatus Center at George Mason University, Jason J. Fichtner of the LIMRA Retirement Income Institute and Kathryn Anne Edwards of the National Academy of Social Insurance.

Johnson previewed the hearing in an X $TWTR post on Tuesday, according to Benzinga. He called Social Security a "legal Ponzi scheme" and wrote that the hearing would lay out how the program has been "grossly mismanaged."

In a video attached to the post, Johnson said the money workers paid into the program was spent "the minute it came in the door," according to Benzinga. He argued that the government could have put program surpluses into investments from 1986 onward, leaving $14.5 trillion in "hard assets" available for paying out benefits.

Nancy Altman, president of the advocacy group Social Security Works, criticized the remarks in a statement, according to MarketWatch. "Millions of seniors and people with disabilities will face homelessness and hunger" if the program were shut down, she said.

Social Security’s trust funds are on track to be exhausted within six years, according to the Congressional Budget Office, as cited by MarketWatch. If that happens, payments to recipients could be reduced by roughly 26%, according to the CBO. Congress has yet to settle on a plan for closing the gap, MarketWatch said.

AARP published a report earlier this month on how shifting income patterns have shortened the program's projected solvency. Social Security's trustees project the combined trust fund will run out in 2034, and AARP said the share of GDP subject to the payroll tax fell from 41% to 34% in 2025. AARP said that had the share held steady, the program could cover scheduled benefits in full through 2059.

Gordon Ramsay Turns Up the Heat: Why He’s ‘Allergic’ to Air Fryers, Hates the Word ‘Famous’ and Thinks Paris Is the Most Overrated Food City

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Before Gordon Ramsay, culinary television was calm.

Smiling chefs like Julia Child and Emeril Lagasse brought restaurant cooking into the average household, gently coaxing viewers to be more adventurous at home. Nary a dish was chucked, nor an F-bomb flung.

Then a blond-haired Brit with a hot temper and a potty mouth burst onto the scene with the docuseries “Boiling Point,” which followed him into the kitchen as he chased his third Michelin star. The show made Ramsay a celebrity and laid the foundation for the future of culinary entertainment. Related Stories Gal Gadot Says Skydance Co-CEO Ynon Kreiz 'Gives Me Hope' Against Antisemitism: 'I’m Frightened by How Quickly Hatred of Jews Is Spreading' ‘Musk’ Doc Gets International Release Dates

In the years that followed, Ramsay built a television empire on “Kitchen Nightmares” and “Hell’s Kitchen.” He didn’t bring unforgiving intensity to fine-dining kitchens; rather, he showed the world what those kitchens were already like. Ditching the cook-along format that dominated the Food Network, Ramsay created pure primetime drama, turning the kitchen into a delicious deathmatch.

Meanwhile, his restaurant business exploded. With 17 Michelin stars under his belt, Ramsay has roughly 100 restaurants operating across the globe.

Ramsay’s entertainment legacy will be cemented — literally — on the Hollywood Walk of Fame on Nov. 18. So, the famous chef called Variety to discuss his career (and why he hates the word “famous”) the day after the Democrats claimed both chambers of Congress in a historic midterms sweep.

“I’m sure you woke up with a big smile on your face,” said Ramsay, who typically avoids discussing politics. “It’s extraordinary. It’s everything that everyone has been talking about for the last 18 months. I’m just pleased that it’s arrived, that redemption.” Without naming Donald Trump, Ramsay described the so-called “blue wave” as “exactly what someone deserves — and about time.”

Over the phone, Ramsay is especially engaged and chatty. He’s warm and friendly, even when berating me over a certain kitchen appliance.

We just finished the new season of “Kitchen Nightmares,” which airs in January. We started off in Minnesota, then we went to Kansas and finished up in Nashville — that backbone of Middle America. It was two of the most exciting months I’ve ever spent, protecting the legacies of these family restaurants, some of which have been running for 50 years.

You start to understand just how tiny the U.K. is in comparison to the incredible melting pot of America. The one thing I connected with immediately was the drive and the hard work, and what these restaurants stand for in the community, whether it’s a strong Polish community or South Korean, Italian, Irish. The notion that Americans are laid back and take it for granted is absolute rubbish. I’ve never felt so inspired to see how much hard work goes in. I grew up in a privileged national healthcare system where it was free, and so I didn’t quite realize how much some of these communities are on the back foot — parents sacrificing their diabetes medicine to keep the restaurant lights on because that’s more important than keeping themselves alive. So, it’s a mixed emotion, but it is one thing I’ve understood from day one: America is a hard-working nation, and if you want to make it, put the hours in. It will happen.

Honestly, from Michelin to Emmys to BAFTAs, I think this is the most nerve-racking because when I first came to the U.S., I came to L.A. for a weekend in the summer of 1994 to watch the World Cup final. The day before the game, I was on the Hollywood Walk of Fame. I was a hardworking young chef — 26, 27 years of age — and I could not believe walking down that strip and seeing the names in that pavement and taking photographs on an old black-and-white camera. It’s a full-circle moment because I was refusing to walk on those stones — I was walking around them to get the proper pictures and to see how polished they were.

I’ll be honest with you, I never use that word. I dislike the word “famous.” I always say “busy.” When I started getting attention, it was a little bit embarrassing, because all you’re doing is your job, right? But I remember turning up for my first book signing, and there was a line outside the door, and I was driving past the bookshop, Waterstones, and I said, “Who are those people there for?” My publicist at the time said, “They’re for you.” And then I thought, “Holy shit! I had no idea there was going to be a queue outside the door.”

I don’t know why, but it makes me uncomfortable. I suppose because of the background I grew up in — I never liked to brag. I’m busy, and I’d rather use the word “busy” than “famous.” I know that sounds a bit weird, but that’s how I feel.

It’s a shame, isn’t it? I am time-poor, but I’m also very time-smart. I don’t waste time. I’ve had to turn down some incredible requests to judge a finale of “MasterChef” in India, or to go to Brazil and run a live “Next Level Chef” finale, appear on “The Traitors” or learn how to dance on “Dancing With the Stars.” I had to say no a few years back to hosting Shark Week. I’m obsessed with sharks; I was so freaking excited. Unfortunately, I couldn’t fit it in my diary.

What I have learned is how to create unscripted drama. I can put you in a scenario tomorrow where a restaurant is running perfectly. And then I’ll take you to the area where something is about to unfold. I read situations, almost like a psychic, five minutes before they happen because I can see what’s happening — from the kitchen’s perspective to the dining room to the customer feedback. That’s what I’ve mastered over the last 20 years. And that’s not overproducing moments. That’s knowing the industry so well that I can give you the points of jeopardy every five minutes in every area.

God, that’s a tough word: rage. I’m going to bring it back to passion. I’m sorry to my mother that it looks like I’m angry. To my next-door neighbor, it’s like, “Christ Almighty, why is he so upset?” It is absolute passion. Cooking is exactly the same as live sports. If you had to listen to an NFL player mic’d up going in for that tackle or throwing an 80-yard pass, it’d be the exact same.

I’m quintessentially blunt; I’ll get it off my chest. I’ll get straight to the point, and I deal with things immediately because I know what kind of shit’s coming down the track next.

I don’t think it’s changed. I was watching “SAS: Who Dares Wins” recently on Fox, and I was looking at those poor celebrities. They were chained to a fucking car, and the car was sunk underwater. I don’t think that shit’s changed. Plus, sports have gotten more intense. I was at a hockey game recently in L.A. Have you seen the way those players beat the shit out of each other?

There was a five-year-old screaming, “Knock his fucking teeth out, rip his ears off!” So, let’s not get too synthetic. Let’s hold onto that hunger in sports and hold on to that hunger in TV.

I’ve moved with the times, right? In Season 25 of “Hell’s Kitchen,” I’m dealing with young individuals that grew up watching that show. I think about how there were periods of vacancy in their life, because COVID, they weren’t too sure where they were going. Now they’re 25, and I help get them on the straight and narrow. I push them to the extreme because I want the best out of them. And I say to them at the beginning of that competition, “Hey, you’re going to love me, you’re going to hate me, you’re going to laugh, you’re going to cry, but you’re going to have the best coach you’ve ever fucking had in your entire career. Use it, learn from it, and step up from it. And don’t take it personally; take it professionally.”

Do you have any idea how many times I’ve been to people’s houses and I’ve heard, “Hey, Joe and Rachel, your food’s amazing. You should open a restaurant.” I’m like, “Holy shit, no! Do not open a fucking restaurant on the back of one successful dinner party because you cooked for 25 people and eat fried turkey at fucking Thanksgiving. Don’t do that, Ethan!”

To round it off, I actually worked in my industry. It’s not as if I did two years in it and then said, “Fuck it, I’m going to be on TV.” I’m a real chef that happens to run multiple restaurants across the globe but also has an incredible level of knowledge coming into these shows. I remember being in Vegas for the first time after Season 1 of “Hell’s Kitchen,” and this guy came up to me and asked me for a picture. He then asked me, “Where did I learn?” I said, “Oh, I lived in Paris, and I trained with Marco.” He said, “You trained where, Marco? Who?” I said, “Marco Pierre White.” He said, “I thought you went to acting school.” I said, “Excuse me?” He said, “You’re acting.” I said, “Fucking say that again!” He actually thought I was an actor, and that was the biggest kick in the nuts in my entire life.

Because that [intensity] is what running a kitchen is like to achieve that level of utter perfection. No disrespect, but I’m not a big fan of all those old boring philosophers that want to bullshit how perfect the kitchens are run. That’s not true. Behind the scenes, when the shit hits the fan, you need to clear that fan. It wasn’t all about tweezers and bloody edible flowers.

They’ve blown up big time. There’s a lot of excitement around their content, but their saving grace is no one ever gets to taste their fucking food. Cooking one dish, editing it and going viral in front of millions is completely different than cooking 65 dishes every night in a restaurant.

I’m OK with it as long as it’s not too much of an interruption and a discomfort to other guests. But I was at the Grand Prix on Sunday in Mexico, and I was on the start line and watching those cars go off. I swear to God, I looked around the stadium with 25,000 people — 24,950 of them had their phones out. I’m like, “No, just watch it fucking live! Put your phone down.”

Oh, slop bowls, yes. I see kids go surfing down in Malibu, and the first thing they pick up is a Chipotle bowl for the beach. They are delicious.

I break out in a severe fucking rash. You can’t get decent color in an air fryer.

No color, no flavor. Ethan, Jesus Christ! I developed the most amazing hybrid technology non-stick pan. I’m going to send it to you. Can I send you some pans?

I’m going to get your details after this. I’m gonna send you some incredible HexClad pans.

No foam on coffee. I’ve seen them do parmesan foam on top of a pasta dish. There is nothing worse. It looks like toxic scum on a stagnant pool. No thank you.

Please tell me you haven’t got an air fryer.

Oh, for fuck’s sake, Ethan, you’re better than that!

Winterize Your Car Before the First Freeze – Start With a Free Battery Test

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Before the first cold snap, have your car’s battery tested free at an auto parts store, check tire tread and pressure, top up antifreeze and winter washer fluid, and replace worn wipers. Then pack an emergency kit for the trunk in case you’re stranded anyway.

Cold weather lowers a battery’s power just as the engine needs more of it to start, so a battery that starts the car slowly in October can fail on the first frigid morning.

AutoZone and O’Reilly Auto Parts test batteries free, right in the car. Replacing a weak one now costs only a battery you’d soon need anyway, while waiting until it fails adds a jump-start or tow, plus a late start to work.

Where snow and ice are routine, switch to winter tires, which keep their grip below about 45 degrees, when all-season tires stiffen. Your regular tires don’t wear while they’re stored, so they last more seasons. If you’ll keep the car a few years, put the winter set on its own wheels, so each spring and fall swap skips paying a shop to remount the tires. Where snow is occasional, all-weather tires can stay on all year, and unlike most all-season tires they carry the mountain-and-snowflake symbol for severe snow.

Stock the trunk with an ice scraper, a blanket, a flashlight, jumper cables or a jump starter, kitty litter for traction, water, snacks, and a phone charger. Save a roadside assistance number in your phone, and decide whether a AAA membership is worth it.

Drivers stuck in snow should stay with the car, keep the tailpipe clear so fumes can’t back up inside, and run the engine about 10 minutes an hour for heat, with a window cracked.

Israel launches new ground operation in Gaza

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Israel has said its military is targeting Hamas infrastructure in central Gaza and urged civilians to avoid the area. The fight is unfolding against the backdrop of a dire food and shelter situation for Palestinians.

Israel says its military is involved in operations on Wednesday in central Gaza in an effort to dislodge Hamas militants.

France's AFP news agency reported "intense Israeli artillery shelling amid ongoing gunfire and airstrikes."

A statement from the Israeli military (IDF) said, "The IDF launched a precise operation within Gaza earlier today west of the Yellow Line to remove a threat posed to IDF troops operating in the Gaza Strip."

The Yellow Line in Gaza was established in an October 2025 ceasefire between Israel and Hamas, and separates Israeli and Hamas-controlled territory.

On Wednesday, COGAT, an Israeli military body responsible for civilian matters in the Palestinian territories warned international aid agencies and Palestinian civilians to avoid areas near the Al-Bureij and Nuseirat refugee camps and Gaza's main thoroughfare, the Salah al-Din road.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Palestinian medics told Reuters news agency that one person had been killed in an Israeli airstrike in central Gaza and that two more had been killed in separate incidents unfolding in Deir al-Balah and Gaza City.

Hamas — which is listed as a terrorist organization by Israel, the US, Germany, the EU and others — blasted the Israeli operation, calling it "a flagrant violation of the ceasefire agreement and confirmation that the occupation has reneged on its obligations."

Israel's military countered that it was reacting to Hamas ceasefire violations and the group's attempts to rearm.

The October 2025 ceasefire halted major operations, but both sides have continued to carry out limited attacks. Gaza health authorities report at least 1,600 Palestinian deaths and Israel reports the loss of fewer than 10 soldiers since the ceasefire went into effect.

In all, Palestinian authorities say more than 74,000 people have been killed inside Gaza during Israel's retaliation for Hamas' October 7, 2023, assault — which killed roughly 1,200 Israelis, most of whom were civilians.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Beyond the threat of violent gunfire and airstrikes, civilians in Gaza continue to face ongoing food insecurity and lack of adequate shelter as winter approaches.

On Tuesday, the International Organization for Migration (IOM) and the UN's World Food Programme (WFP) warned that funding shortfalls and access restrictions put untold numbers of Palestinian lives at risk.

Currently, more than 1.7 million Gazans are displaced, with IOM and WFP saying 1.4 million face acute hunger and 1.98 million require shelter assistance.

With Gaza's infrastructure decimated by years of Hamas mismanagement and pounding Israeli attacks, more than 80% of Palestinians are unemployed, meaning they cannot afford food and have been forced to burn garbage to try to keep warm.

"Families in Gaza are facing their first sleepless nights of a very long winter ahead," said WFP Palestine Country Director Shaun Hughes. "Unless we get immediate support to scale up the humanitarian response, we risk this becoming not only a shelter crisis but also a deeper food security crisis."

New York Style Has Always Been and Always Will be About a Great Jacket

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

When it comes to New York style, each neighborhood is its own microcosm. The It-girl DJs of Dimes Square are sartorially miles away from the midtown men zipped into their puffer vests (even if the finance-bro boyfriend, fashionista girlfriend is a common romantic pairing). But once the weather starts to chill, there’s one item that unites New Yorkers above all, and that’s a fabulous jacket.

A dip below 40 degrees can set off alarm bells for the start of seasonal depression elsewhere, but not here in the Big Apple. Last Friday’s chilly rain did little to stop the procession of on-trend faux furs I saw out and about in Chinatown, Williamsburg, and Park Slope. When you finally get to wear your statement coat the elements are irrelevant.

Recent news cycles affirm the mind games that a great jacket can play. Much has been written about former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s penchant for luxury leather, but the person with the real kink is the one who photoshopped him into Hedi Slimane-era Saint Laurent. Conner Ives’s faux-fur trimmed silk robe coat is stirring up almost as much fervor, with fashionistas everywhere trying to recreate the look, vintage or otherwise.

Try it on for size: Maybe you’re having a great day and throw on a pastel mohair overcoat for some extra pep in your step, or perhaps you’re in a bit of a slump and slip into your favorite buttery leather bomber to collect a few mood-boosting compliments. Vogue’s senior editor Joan Kennedy walked into the office on Monday in a seriously sensational velvet Victorian jacket; after two compliments in quick succession, she confided that’s exactly why she wore it: to brighten up the start of the week.

Dare to forgo your outerwear flair and you’ll see envy-inducing toppers everywhere. My camera roll is filled with pics of perfectly cropped tweeds (not the granny kind), elegantly draped trenches, and an antique-looking jacquard duster that almost made me chase its wearer down the Columbus Circle escalator.

As one user on X so aptly noted: “Everything in the world is about jackets, except jackets. Jackets are about power.” And in New York, there’s no greater one-up than a damn good one.

Your Favorite Coffee Shop Is Probably Streaming Music Illegally. It’s the Music Industry’s Fault, Too (Guest Column)

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A new study found 81% of U.S. businesses don't properly license music, costing creators and other rightsholders. The fix starts with education — not lawsuits.

Walk into almost any shop, gym, salon or restaurant in America, and you’ll hear music. Ask the owner whether it’s licensed and you will likely get a confident “yes.” But in most cases, that answer is, unfortunately, wrong.

Soundtrack recently surveyed 1,525 decision-makers responsible for nearly 15,000 business locations across 10 customer-facing industries to find out how U.S. businesses actually play music. Though the law is clear that businesses need specific licenses to play music in public, the results of the survey describe a market that loves music, depends on it, and, overwhelmingly, does not properly pay for it.

Ninety-seven percent of the businesses we surveyed play music for customers, and 77% do it every day. Adjusted for the size of the U.S., that means roughly 3.8 million U.S. business locations play music in their stores.

Nine in 10 of those surveyed said music boosts employee motivation and shapes the energy of their space, while more than eight in 10 called it an essential part of the business and said it has to fit their brand. Rather than merely being a background expense, businesses see music as part of the product.

Yet despite this overwhelming demand, the way music played in businesses is paid for has not evolved. A striking 79% of businesses illicitly use a consumer streaming service, such as a personal Spotify, Pandora or YouTube account, to play music for customers. Only 28% of businesses use a service built and licensed for business. This is one of the biggest failures, and also one of the biggest opportunities, for the music industry today.

Aside from the scale of misuse, it is significant how many businesses are sure they’re doing it right. Seventy-eight percent of respondents were confident the music in their business is legally compliant. Eighty-three percent said they’re familiar with the rules. Yet 81% are misusing music, and 58% believe it’s legal to stream a personal account in their business.

Even I struggle to distill the U.S. licensing rules into just a few sentences; such is the unnecessary complexity surrounding them. Put simply, businesses require both a music service licensed for commercial use and licenses with all four U.S. performing rights organizations (PROs) — ASCAP, BMI, SESAC and GMR — that cover the music they play.

Some licensed music services bundle performance rights, while others require businesses to secure separate public performance licenses from U.S. PROs. For a business owner, working out exactly what’s included can be a job in itself.

Too often I speak to business owners who believe they’re fully licensed but are missing key PRO licenses or still playing music via their personal streaming account. The survey reflects this, finding only 5% of businesses hold licenses from all four U.S. PROs.

However, this isn’t a story about bad actors. Nine in 10 businesses said compliance matters to them, and most said it matters that artists are paid fairly. The businesses using personal streaming accounts are doing it because it’s what they know. It’s easy, familiar and often free. It’s not a problem of intent; it’s a lack of information and a non-user-friendly system.

The cost to music creators and other rightsholders is real. Combining what businesses told us they spend on music with the number of establishments recorded by the Bureau of Labor Statistics, we estimate that annual spending on music would rise by about $1.4 billion per year if every business was properly licensed through a compliant business music service. That money should flow to songwriters, artists, publishers, labels and the services that license them legally.

The music industry shares the blame. No one proactively educates the market or gives a business owner a clear answer about what they need to be fully covered. Consumer platforms offer free tiers, teaching a generation of shop owners that music costs nothing. And business music services, mine included, have not explained the difference loudly or simply enough.

Enforcement alone won’t close a gap this wide. You can’t sue three million locations into compliance, and you shouldn’t want to. These are the customers who say music is essential to them. The answer is a coordinated push across trade organisations, PROs, publishers, labels, consumer platforms and business services to create one plain-language message about what is legal, provide simpler routes to full licensing and build services businesses actually want to use.

That last part matters. Businesses told us what they look for in streaming services designed for businesses: ease-of-use, price and good playlists (the main complaint about business music services is that their music selections are limited and repetitive). The music offered by these services has to be at least as good as the consumer app on the owner’s smartphone — or the consumer app will keep winning.

Streaming rebuilt the recorded music industry by making the legal option easier for individuals than piracy. Now, the same needs to happen for businesses. The demand is already there, and most want to do it right. What’s missing is clarity, and a legal option that’s simple to use and offers better selections.

That $1.4 billion isn’t lost. It’s waiting for the industry to make doing the right thing the easy thing.

Ola Sars is the co-founder and CEO of Soundtrack, the music streaming service for businesses. The study was conducted by Soundtrack and the NMPA in 2025, surveying 1,525 U.S. music decision-makers via Sago’s opt-in B2B panel.

Your email address will not be published. Required fields are marked *

A daily briefing on what matters in the music industry

Sky Sports: Man Utd Plotting £67m Move for Newcastle Star Malick Thiaw

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Manchester United are looking to bolster their defensive ranks in 2027, and a new target has emerged as a possibility for Mauricio Pochettino.

The Argentine has replaced Michael Carrick as the boss at Old Trafford this week, and will lead the team into a new era, with a defensive rebuild planned for the new year in both January and the summer.

Harry Maguire, Luke Shaw, and Lisandro Martinez are all out of contract at the end of the current campaign, and it seems unlikely that any of them will be staying at the club beyond this season. New arrivals are expected to take their place, and the club are once again looking at Premier League players who can come in to make an instant impact.

A number of central defenders have been linked with a January move to the club, including Brentford captain Nathan Collins and Fulham star Calvin Bassey.

But it seems United are in contact with a number of players at the Unique Sports Group agency, who represent Bassey, as a report by Sky Sports Germany states that the club are also showing a strong interest in Newcastle United star Malick Thiaw.

The 25-year-old joined the Magpies in the summer of 2025 from AC Milan in a deal worth around £30m, and immediately established himself as a key player. His status in the squad was cemented this summer, when he was appointed into the club's leadership group, and he has worn the captain's armband on a number of occasions this season.

Thiaw is now valued at £67m by Newcastle, but it's reported that a summer exit in 2027 has not been ruled out, with Man Utd among the most interested parties for his signature.

Regarded as the 'undisputed leader' of the defence at St James' Park, the club do not want to lose him, but accept that they may be forced to sell for a big fee if Champions League clubs come calling, and they are unable to turn their slow start to the season around.

The 6ft 4 star has also recently broken into the Germany first-team, with Jurgen Klopp understood to be a huge fan of his qualities. His current contract is due to expire in 2029.

Man Utd need defensive reinforcements, and so it's no surprise that a number of players are going to start being linked with a move to Old Trafford as a result.

INEOS have shown that they prefer to shop in the Premier League since taking over, and Thiaw would fit that. He is also just 25 and has his prime years ahead of him, while he has the experience of playing for a big club like AC Milan in his career too.

But with Newcastle likely to demand a huge fee to let him leave, the Red Devils could surely find a player of comparable quality for a much lesser fee, and free up funds to make other signings instead.

Thiaw has his qualities, but we have seen him exposed in the Premier League before. Until there is a leader and proven starter in the backline to help guide him along, United would be making a huge gamble by spending such big money on bringing him in at this stage of their rebuild under Pochettino.

United simply cannot afford to get their signings wrong at this stage, and with Newcastle certain to play hardball for any deal, they'd be better off looking elsewhere unless they are 100% convinced he is the right man for the role and the new manager.

Alaska Airlines Eyes New Long-Haul Boeing 787 Route: But There's A Major Twist

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Alaska Airlines is keen to fly to Lisbon, which is the capital of Portugal and an important destination for tourism. The oneworld member has previously expressed interest in flying there. A major ongoing stumbling block is the lack of slots, at least until, and if, Lisbon's new airport opens in many years.

As Ishrion Aviation has pointed out, Alaska Airlines applied for slots for a daily service to Lisbon. Unsurprisingly, it was not granted any of them. Slots do exist, but only in limited numbers and at particular times. It is likely that the growing US operator would either have to reduce its request substantially (say, to a logical three weekly flights) or accept inferior times, as WestJet did. Given Alaska Airlines' hub structure, a poor schedule would not be sensible.

The carrier has big plans for its long-haul network, although it is finding the going tough for now. Recently, it called its long-haul routes 'a drag', but they'd always take time to develop, even before the rise in the price of fuel. Against this backdrop, it'd be reasonable to question the appeal of potential future flights between Seattle, obviously Alaska Airlines' primary hub, and the Portuguese capital.

In the 12 months to September 2026, booking data via OAG shows that 22,700 round-trip passengers flew indirectly between the two cities. Obviously, that volume is small, although nonstop flights, along with hopefully strong promotions, would easily grow that by 40% to 50% in the first season.

Nonetheless, the supposed daily frequency would be highly excessive (three weekly flights would make much more sense). As such, it would therefore rely even more than usual on lower-yielding transfer traffic via Seattle.

Despite the low existing traffic, Lisbon is Seattle's third-largest unserved European market, excluding places like Athens, which Alaska Airlines has already announced. Zurich was the top unserved market (36,100 passengers), which stopped having nonstop flights with the Swiss leisure operator Edelweiss in 2025, followed by the oneworld hub of Madrid (25,500).

Of course, traffic (both existing and likely stimulated) is just one part of it. Across all airlines, cabins, and passengers, the average fare to Lisbon was $641 one-way (excluding a fuel surcharge, taxes, and add-ons). That was similar to Barcelona, to which Delta Air Lines began flying this year, almost certainly to get in before Alaska Airlines announced the route. However, Lisbon flights would cover less distance and be less expensive to serve.

The airline's international expansion continues.

More notably, and perhaps worryingly, Seattle-Athens had an average one-way fare of $646, which was less than 1% higher than Lisbon's. However, flights to the capital of Greece, which Alaska Airlines will begin on May 12, 2027, will cover 19% more distance and be much more expensive to operate. Of course, in these instances, and elsewhere where it is the only operator, it should get a reasonable price premium for the convenience of flying nonstop.

The carrier's debut to Europe took place in April 2026, when the first flight from Seattle to Rome took off. The launch came after Alaska Airlines began its first-ever long-haul service, which was to Asia. The first route was from Washington State to Tokyo Narita in May 2025, followed by Seoul Incheon four months later.

Alaska Airlines' Planned Operations To Europe: July 2027

Faces British Airways, Delta, and Virgin Atlantic

After Rome came London Heathrow (May 2026), followed by Reykjavík (May 2026). Next year, Alaska Airlines will begin flying to Athens (May 2027) and Paris CDG (May 2027). When writing, the carrier currently plans five transatlantic routes, with a big emphasis on leisure markets, just like WestJet from Canada to Europe. This is perhaps why Alaska Airlines has not announced flights to Madrid, in what would be a logical tie-up with fellow oneworld member Iberia.

Let's narrow the focus to mid-July 2027. In that week, Alaska Airlines will be one of 12 airlines flying from Seattle to Europe, which will collectively have 147 weekly departures (21 daily). Discover Airlines will be there, as the Lufthansa Group's leisure unit is replacing Lufthansa itself. Alaska Airlines will have 28 weekly departures (four daily) and rank second behind Delta (35 weekly; five daily).

A Bear Market Is Coming Eventually. History Says This Is the Best Investing Move You Can Make.

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A bear market is coming eventually, but history suggests the bigger risk to your wealth is how you react when it arrives.

By John Ballard – Nov 18, 2026 at 1:15PM EST

Bear markets are a regular part of investing. Since 1928, the S&P 500 has fallen into one -- a drop of at least 20% from a recent high -- about every 3.5 years, on average, according to Hartford Funds and Ned Davis Research. The typical bear market has erased about 35% of the index's value.

That sounds frightening, but bear markets are usually short. The average one has lasted about 9.6 months. These downturns are less a disaster than they are the cost of admission to building long-term wealth. The bigger risk is how investors react when the next one arrives.

For anyone with many years until retirement, here's why I'd stay fully invested.

According to Hartford and Ned Davis, the S&P 500 has had 28 bull markets and 27 bear markets since 1928. Through all those ups and downs, it delivered a compound annual return of about 10%, according to The Motley Fool's research. The market rises more in a bull market than it loses in a bear market.

It pays to keep investing when things look worst. Capital Group tracked the 18 biggest market declines since the Great Depression and found the S&P 500 was higher five years later every time. MFS found that after the 10 worst calendar years since 1975, a $100,000 investment was worth about $207,000 on average five years later.

Don't think that you'll be better off waiting to invest until the market moves higher. In fact, history says you'll be wealthier by staying fully invested through every down year.

J.P. Morgan Asset Management found that $10,000 invested in the S&P 500 at the start of 2006 grew to $80,619 by the end of 2025 for someone who stayed fully invested. However, missing just the 10 best days cut that to $35,866.

Take 2025: The S&P 500 fell nearly 19% in the spring, yet its best day came on April 9, in the middle of the turmoil. Missing that one day would have cut the year's 17.9% return to 7.6%.

Now, some will think: "I'll just sit in cash and wait until a down year to buy bargains and make a fortune." That's not a smart strategy either. Bear markets are infrequent. Over the past 95 years, stocks have been rising about 78% of the time, according to Hartford.

Bear markets are also hard to predict. A normal pullback of 10% or so can easily be mistaken for the start of a bigger decline.

Waiting for the perfect moment tends to backfire. Morningstar found that the average dollar in U.S. mutual funds and exchange-traded funds (ETFs) earned 8.7% a year over the decade ended 2025, versus 9.9% for the funds themselves, largely because of when investors bought and sold.

A bear market is, indeed, coming, but we don't know when. The best move is to stay invested and not sell when the market goes down, because missing the biggest up days after the bottom will cost you a lot more money down the road than if you just stayed invested.

Time in the market is far more important than market timing. From 1970 through September 2026, every 20-year rolling period for the S&P 500 produced a gain, according to Mellon data published by Ave Maria Mutual Funds.

Even the worst timing can still make money for patient investors. Hartford calculated that investing $10,000 at the peak just before each of the six bear markets since 1995 would have still turned $60,000 into $364,774 by the end of 2025. That beats staying in cash and earning almost nothing.

As Warren Buffett wrote in his 1991 shareholder letter, "The stock market serves as a relocation center at which money is moved from the active to the patient."

The best time to start is always when you have money available. Pick a low-cost index fund such as the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO -0.10%), which tracks the S&P 500, or the Vanguard Total Stock Market ETF (VTI -0.02%), which covers the whole U.S. market.

Set up an automatic purchase of a fixed amount each month or with each paycheck, and keep it running no matter what happens in the markets. Keep money you'll need in the next few years out of stocks, and you'll be on your way to growing your net worth.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 794%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 203% for the S&P 500.

They just revealed what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy right now, available when you join Stock Advisor.

Sources: Rangers have rejected ‘big’ Djeidi Gassama offers as ‘only way’ for exit revealed

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Transfer Correspondent AUTHORITY Former Sky Sports and ESPN FC correspondent who specialises in global recruitment, contract negotiations, and high-velocity transfer breaking news. FOCUS Exclusive reporting, domestic and international transfer markets, and elite-level insights. THE INSIGHT Pete utilises a Global Network of Industry Sources to deliver verified, first-to-market reporting. His “Deep-Lens” analysis connects the dots between boardroom strategy and pitch-level recruitment, providing a level of transfer clarity that goes beyond surface-level rumours.

Rangers have no intention of cashing in on winger Djeidi Gassama in January amid interest from Monza, sources have told Football Insider.

The Italian side are the latest club to take an interest in the 23-year-old, but Derek McInnes has no doubt that he is not for sale.

Rangers are understood to have turned down “big offers” for the former Sheffield Wednesday man in previous transfer windows, and still view him as a key part of their long-term plans.

Sources say that the “only way” they would let Gassama out of the door in January is if an offer came in that was too good for them to refuse.

However, that is not expected to come from Monza, who are struggling at the foot of the Serie A table at the moment.

Rangers fully expect that Gassama will still be part of their squad in the second half of the season, and they are happy for that scenario to play out.

Gassama endured an inconsistent start to life at Ibrox last term, but still managed to notch nine goals and five assists in all competitions.

He already has three assists in the Premiership so far this term, and is currently winning the battle for regular minutes in Rangers’ wide areas.

McInnes is blessed with a wealth of options on the flanks, with Gassama, Findlay Curtis, Minsu Kim, Badredine Bouanani and Daisuke Yokota all pushing for starts.

However, it is Gassama who is emerging at the top of that group at the moment and appears to be winning the boss over with his performances.

That has reduced any chance of him being sold even further in recent weeks, with McInnes viewing him as a potential difference-maker for his team.

Football Insider revealed on Wednesday that Dinamo Bucharest winger Danny Armstrong is keen to return to Scotland and join Rangers in January.

The 29-year-old is flourishing in Romania, and already has 16 goal involvements from just 15 games in the top flight this season.

Sources say that Armstrong would find it “hard to turn down” a move to Ibrox this winter, and is keen to reunite with McInnes.

He previously played under the Gers boss at Kilmarnock, where he became a firm favourite of the manager.
