11 unresolved mysteries FROM must answer in 5th and final season

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

From the town's origins to red lightning, 11 open FROM mysteries the final season must settle, and which ones the ending may not need.

Viewers of "From" have spent four seasons watching people trapped in a town nobody can leave, under rules nobody can explain, and each season has raised new questions faster than it has answered the old ones. Those questions now have a deadline. The series has been renewed for a fifth season that will be its last, ScreenRant reports, and that season is expected in 2027, according to Entertainment Weekly. After a long wait, the writers will have a single season to explain every unexplained rule, creature and power, or to decide to leave it alone. The open questions reach well beyond how the town began and into how it works from one night to the next. The number of them, more than any single riddle, is what keeps the ending uncertain.

The show will end at the length its makers first proposed, though that outcome was not settled along the way. ScreenRant reports that the series was pitched to MGM+ as five seasons and 50 episodes. In 2023, during the show's second season, executive producer Jeff Pinkner was weighing four, five or six seasons, according to Arizona Daily Star. The fifth and final season brings the series back to its original plan after a period when that plan was one option among several. Pinkner's view at the time was that when a series is built around its characters, the people making it can sense when it should stop. The makers will measure the ending by where the characters arrive, not by how many puzzles get closed. A fan's checklist and the finale may not match, and some threads may be answered only as far as the characters need them to be. That gap sets the fairest test for any open question: whether the ending depends on it, not whether it is intriguing.

FandomWire set out to find which open puzzles the show must solve to conclude its final season, from the red lightning to Julie's storywalking, the Boy in White and the Lake of Tears. Those puzzles are less separate than they look. In the fourth season's finale, the writers brought many of them into a single night and tied them to the same antagonist, so answering one may answer several. Some have already been partly settled on screen, while others grew more urgent after the town lost its protections, and "unresolved" means something different from one question to the next. Each slide that follows weighs what a question still hides and why the ending needs it answered.

Most of the show's riddles concern events inside Fromville, but this one concerns the town itself, and every other answer depends on it. Before the series ends, it has to say who made the town, what ties it to the world beyond and what becomes of the place and everything in it if the cycle ends, according to FandomWire. Those are the most basic facts about any place, and the show has yet to supply any of them. A finale can leave a side story unresolved, but a finale that never says what the town is would leave every other explanation without a basis. This is also the answer most likely to settle several questions at once, because the identity of the town's maker and the nature of its tie to the outside world would bear on nearly every mystery that grew out of them.

The confusion the residents feel is what drives the drama, and the people making the show treat that confusion as something to protect. Harold Perrineau, the actor who plays one of the town's leaders, has chosen not to find out how the series ends. "The torture (my character) is feeling is easier for me to embody if I really don’t know," Perrineau told the Arizona Daily Star. That gives the final season a harder job than delivering an explanation, since revealing what the town is will also end the uncertainty behind much of the characters' suffering.

The thing to watch, then, is not only who built Fromville but whether the show's makers reveal it in a way that gives that suffering a purpose rather than simply ending it.

The bargain behind Fromville is still one of the show's most basic open questions, and the final season will have to say who made it and how it works. FandomWire counts the sacrifice among the unanswered questions about the town's origins that the final season has to take up. Part of the trade is known: The original townspeople gave up seven children and received immortality in return, according to ComicBook.com. The town's founding was therefore a deliberate atrocity by a whole community, and any moral reckoning in the last season has to answer for it. Knowing the price is not the same as understanding the deal, though, and the show has yet to explain how the bargain was struck or what keeps it in force.

The fourth season's finale showed how little the residents understand about what the founders set in motion. Boyd and other townspeople uprooted the Bottle Tree, ComicBook.com reports, and a crimson lightning storm appeared over the town after the retrieval went wrong. The residents acted without knowing the rules behind the place, and they now face a storm none of them can explain. A question about the town's origins has become a pressing problem for the people living there now.

The residents still can't explain the bargain, so the way out may not lie in the children's story alone. The sacrifice now belongs to the larger struggle between the Man in Yellow and the Boy in White, and the final season may resolve it through that contest rather than as a separate puzzle.

For the ending, the real test is whether anyone breaks the bargain's hold on the town. A full account of how the founders struck it may matter less, and an ending built around the characters can deliver that kind of answer.

The show has already confirmed that people in Fromville come back, so the open question about reincarnation is no longer whether it happens but what it is for. FandomWire groups the question surrounding Tabitha and Jade with the town's origins and sacrifices. That grouping fits Tabitha, whom the previous season confirmed as a reincarnation of Miranda, according to Primetimer. Explaining the returns may also mean explaining the deaths that come with them, and whether the killings in the town follow a schedule.

The same family arrives in each cycle. Miranda came to town in the 1970s with her son, Victor, and her daughter, Eloise, Primetimer reports, and Tabitha arrived with one son and one daughter. Victor alone came through Miranda's cycle alive, and in an earlier cycle, the original Tabitha's daughter was sacrificed and became an Anghkooey child. In both cycles viewers know, a daughter was lost. If that pattern holds, Tabitha's daughter came into town already in danger, and the ending can't leave that unanswered.

FROM requires a person to die before being reborn, Primetimer writer Kinette Sumadia writes, and she uses that rule to argue that neither Victor nor the Boy in White is a reincarnation. By that reading, Victor never returned at all. He stayed. He also wants Ethan prepared to survive alone, which suggests he expects Miranda's outcome to repeat with Tabitha's family. Sumadia also connects Ethan to the Boy in White, a link the show hasn't confirmed.

The final season has to decide whether anyone can stop the cycle, because that determines whether Tabitha's children face the same split Miranda's did.

Fromville has a history of mass killing that nobody has accounted for, and that history may not be over. FandomWire lists the 1978 massacre as unexplained and says residents may face new purges each time they discover too much. That sets the logic of the show against its own characters. They stay alive by chasing answers, yet the more they uncover, the closer they may come to the next killing. The final season can't file the massacre away as backstory, because the residents face more danger with every mystery they solve.

The fourth season's finale has already shown what removing one resident looks like. Reel 360 reports that Sophia killed Elgin after he prayed, and his death reads as punishment for defying her rather than as bad luck. Sophia is the vessel for the Man in Yellow, so this killing leads straight to the show's central antagonist and not to a stray monster. She may also have been in town long before this season. The old photo Elgin found shows Sophia, which makes her a possible link between the deaths of the past and those of the present, even though nothing yet places her at the 1978 massacre.

The final season may never give the massacre a tidy explanation drawn from the past, and the writers could answer it with a fresh killing instead. Another round of deaths seems as likely a way to resolve the mystery as a scene set in 1978, though the show has committed to neither. The ending depends on this question because it decides whether the danger in Fromville comes from rules the characters can learn, or from one antagonist who punishes anyone who gets too close.

The bloodiest secret in Fromville may be settled not by finding out what happened in 1978, but by whether it happens again.

Two figures appear to stand outside the rules that trap everyone else in Fromville, and the show has explained neither of them. Nobody yet knows who the Man in Yellow and the Boy in White are or what either one wants, and the two differ sharply in how freely they act and how they help the residents, according to FandomWire. The Man in Yellow spent the fourth season disguised as Sophia, living among the residents without their knowing who he was, according to ScreenRant. The Boy in White, by contrast, offers only cryptic guidance, so the residents receive help they can't fully interpret. One of the two moves among the townspeople under a stolen identity, while the other stays at the edges of town.

The fourth season's finale turned these two separate puzzles into a single rivalry. Its final scene placed the Boy in White with Sophia just as Sophia was revealed as the Man in Yellow in disguise, ScreenRant reports, and the meeting was deliberate. Series creator and executive producer John Griffin told ScreenRant the scene was "confirmation that these two figures, who have operated in the background and whom we've only seen on the periphery, are absolutely aware of each other." From here on, any answer about what one of them is has to account for the other.

The difference in their freedom could reflect a choice or a limit, and the show hasn't said which. The Boy in White may keep his distance on purpose, or he may be unable to do more. ScreenRant reports that showrunner and executive producer Jeff Pinkner sees the story as taking shape as it goes rather than following a fixed course. The contest between the two figures is left for the final season to settle. The open question is also a practical one for the characters. How much help the residents can count on before the series ends depends on whether the Boy in White won't act or can't act.

Julie has walked away from the one power in town that lets a person move through the past, and nobody has ever explained how that power works. FandomWire lists her storywalking as unexplained, vanished or changed. ScreenRant reports that Julie gave up on storywalking in the fourth season's ninth episode. The show hasn't said whether the ability faded or changed or whether she simply stopped using it, so the open question now covers Julie as well as the power. With a single season left, the show has to bring the power back on purpose or knowingly let it go.

Whatever storywalking can do, it can't undo every loss in town. Julie can't reverse Jim's death, according to ScreenRant. That limit changes what is at stake each time she goes back. The power may matter more for what Julie could learn than for anything she could change. Casey Duby, a writer and critic at ScreenRant, suggests Julie could look into earlier cycles to expose the Man in Yellow's secrets. In a final season, knowing what happened before may be worth more to the survivors than any chance to erase it.

Julie's doubt now reaches beyond her own ability to the survivors' other plans for fighting back. She argued that Tabitha's plan was a trap, ScreenRant reports, and that suspicion now shapes whom the group is willing to believe. Her doubt places storywalking inside the struggle with the Man in Yellow. Julie has set aside a power that might show his past while the survivors argue over which guidance to follow.

If the ending explains what storywalking is and why Julie gave it up, it could explain a good deal about the Man in Yellow along the way.

In Fromville, characters who die do not necessarily leave the story. Residents the town has lost keep appearing again, from Father Khatri to Jim, and FandomWire counts these returns among the town's entities and supernatural powers, with no explanation for them confirmed. That places the visits closer to a force at work in the town than to grief or memory. Who sends the dead back is as open a question as who they are, and the answer matters more than any list of the names that reappear.

The fourth season's finale suggests the apparitions may belong to a larger mystery. Elgin found an old photo of a young woman who looked just like Sophia, and the finale then revealed that the Man in Yellow had been taking her form, according to Entertainment Weekly. At least one familiar face in town may therefore be borrowed from its past, and recognizing a face no longer proves who is behind it. If the Man in Yellow can take on the appearance of people from the town's history, the returning dead may be his instruments rather than ghosts acting on their own. In that case, the final season could settle the apparitions without addressing them directly, simply by explaining what the Man in Yellow is.

The ending still needs an answer, because the characters can no longer trust what they see. The figure behind Sophia's face can also cloud the minds of the living, and in the finale Sophia drugged Henry and left him hallucinating, according to Entertainment Weekly. Elgin, the resident who came closest to the truth about Sophia's face, died in the same finale, and he now stands among the dead whose possible return is an open question. Any sighting of him in the final season would raise the same doubt he was chasing. A vision in town can be false in two ways, with a dead face being worn or a living mind being clouded.

The finale does not need a catalog of every ghost. It does need a rule for when the dead are really themselves, because the characters cannot make their last choices without knowing whom to trust.

The residents' only proven weapon against a Creature works by rules the show has never explained. Boyd used blood worms to bring down the Smiley Creature, Primetimer reports, but what the worms and the blood did to Boyd and to Henry is still an open question, according to FandomWire. That makes the question a matter of survival, because the residents can't plan a final fight around a weapon whose rules none of them understand. Boyd can't simply strike again, either, because the worms were expelled from his body once he passed them on. Martin carried the worms before Boyd and died while transferring them, so the only man who might have said where they came from is gone. If the town is going to use the worms again, the last season may have to return to the question of where more of them could come from.

The steps of the transfer are known even though its workings are not. "My blood is your blood," Martin said as he handed the worms on, as reported by Primetimer, and Boyd repeated the vow almost word for word when he passed them to Smiley. Boyd copied what he had seen Martin do, but repeating the words is not the same as knowing why a Creature dies when the worms reach it. Until the show reveals those rules, any victory over the Creatures would look like luck rather than a payoff.

Fatima faces the same unknown from the other side, because nobody yet knows whether her change into something monstrous is permanent. The town's rules can destroy a Creature and can also remake a person, and the show has explained neither process.

The worms are a plot device, but Fatima is a character, and whether she can come back is the part of this mystery the final season can least afford to leave open.

Most of the town's open questions ask who or what is out there, but this one asks where. Nobody knows what part the lighthouse or the Lake of Tears plays in breaking the curse, or what would happen if someone brought bones to the lighthouse or the tower, according to FandomWire. That puts escape itself at stake. If the two places do figure in breaking the curse, the residents may be missing a step in the way out of town without knowing what that step does.

The lake entered the story in Ethan's dream in the first season's second episode, Primetimer reports, and for a long time afterward it remained only a dream image. The show never said what it was for, even as it added other mysteries around it. That changed in "Fray," the fourth season's second episode, when Ethan was given the job of finding the lake. The show still hasn't explained what the lake does, but a character is now searching for it on screen. The answer is therefore more likely to come through Ethan's search than through anyone's theories.

Ethan is not the only resident tied to the lake. Victor has a drawing of it in his room at the Colony House, according to Primetimer. Ethan dreamed of a place that already meant something to another person in town. How Victor came to know the lake has not been determined. The lake now holds two questions at once: what it is, and what Victor knows about it. In this last stretch of the show, several questions may share a single answer, and the lake and Victor may turn out to be one of them.

The two landmarks do not carry equal weight in the story. The lake has a dream, an errand and a second resident behind it, while the lighthouse is so far mainly the other half of a question about the curse. For an ending built around where the characters arrive, that difference matters. Ethan is already looking for the lake, so the finale would have a hard time leaving it unexplained. The lighthouse may get only as much explanation as the curse requires, which would make it the half of this mystery that fans keep arguing over longest.

Credit: Mjolnir47542 / MGM+ via From Wiki (Fandom)

Every night in Fromville, residents rely on talismans whose origins nobody can explain. The person or force that crafted the charms, and the source of their power, both lack an answer, according to FandomWire. ScreenRant movie and TV news editor Matthew Rudoy counts the makers and the power source as still unknown as well. Residents would have a hard time rebuilding or repairing a protection whose source they can't name. The charms' origin is therefore a stake in the ending and not a piece of trivia.

Part of the talisman's mystery has already been solved on screen. The meaning of the show's recurring symbols remains on the list of open questions, but ScreenRant reports that Tabitha realized in season 4 that the talisman symbol depicts her and Jade. That answer ties the town's protection to two of its current residents. It still doesn't say who put their image on the charms or why.

The question became urgent in the season 4 finale, when the Man in Yellow took the charms away. Disguised as Sophia, he removed talismans and threw them down a Faraway Tree, according to Reel 360 and ComicBook.com. It isn't clear whether she took most of them or all of them, so the final season also has to settle whether any protection is left. An enemy who went to the trouble of removing the charms likely saw them as a real obstacle, and that makes the question of who built them more pressing.

The red lightning looks like the wildest riddle here, but viewers saw when it began. FandomWire lists what sets it off as unanswered, while ScreenRant places its start right after the bottle tree was pulled up. The show hasn't explained how the tree and the storm are connected. The lightning was not the only upheaval, though. Pulling up the tree also turned the daylight to darkness and shook the ground, and Tabitha and Jade ended up stuck underground in the caves. The lightning is probably one part of that wider disruption, and the show may explain it along with the rest. Sophia reacted to the tree's removal with glee, a sign that worse may be coming for the residents.

The ending doesn't need to decode the lightning so much as account for it: who built the town's defenses, and what the red lightning means for the residents now that those defenses have been taken.

Fromville's daily workings remain as unexplained as its deepest secrets. The exits, the spiders, the music box storyline and the source of the town's electricity and supplies all stay murky, according to FandomWire. The explanations for the electricity and fuel that most Reddit $RDDT users accept are fan opinion, and the show has never confirmed them. Questions such as these rarely move the plot forward. Their answers still decide whether the town feels like a built world or an improvised one, and a final season that skips them risks the second.

Residents receive some necessities and not others, and nobody has explained why. Whatever keeps the town supplied has not brought medical care, because there are no doctors, hospitals or surgeons in town, according to TV Insider. That turns a logistics question into a story question. Any account of how the residents get their supplies would also have to explain why medical care is missing. Such an answer would say something about the nature of the place where these people are trapped.

What the town knows about the Faraway Trees begins with one man. Residents use the name Victor Kavanaugh gave the hollow-trunked bottle trees, and Victor is the town's longest-tenured resident, according to TV Insider. No one in town has seen more of the place than he has. The trees' secret is therefore partly a question of how much Victor knows and how much of it he shares with the others.

The trees may also reach back further than Victor. TV Insider reports that no connection between the trees and the town's former residents has been uncovered yet. If that link exists, the trees belong to the town's history as well as its geography, and to the larger story of who came before and what happened to them.

The power supply is the question the finale can most safely leave to fans, because it has no bearing on whether anyone can get out. The trees and the exits are a different matter. They bear on escape itself and on what the town's longest-tenured resident has kept to himself.

The last season needs to explain the trees, and it can leave the electricity a mystery.

Avelo Airlines Launches 16 New Routes In 4 Weeks: See Full List Now

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Newish airlines tend to grow strongly, benefiting from the revenue, unit cost, market share, and competitive advantages of doing so. For example, five-year-old Breeze Airways' passenger traffic grew by 47% in the 12 months to August. In distinct contrast, Avelo Airlines' passenger numbers fell by 15%.

Avelo has been struggling recently as it seeks to find what works. As such, it has cut many routes, including its entire international operation, and removed multiple served airports from its map. However, the airline, whose fleet consists of 17 Boeing 737-700s and 737-800s, will launch 16 new routes in November and December. Still, compared to the same two months in 2025, 30+ routes have been removed.

Analysis of the airline's schedule submission to Cirium Diio has been explored. This compared the budget operator's network between January 2025 and October 2026 with what is set to operate in November 2026. The process found that the following ten routes will launch. This includes a return to the Caribbean. However, as the two routes will be to Aguadilla, Puerto Rico, they'll be domestic. Aguadilla is a brand-new destination for Avelo.

Half of the entries will be from McKinney (DTX), where Avelo's latest base just opened. Located around 34 miles (54 km) north of downtown Dallas, McKinney's passenger terminal opened on November 11. Not surprisingly, that was exactly when the ultra-low-cost carrier Avelo began flying from the airport. In doing so, the carrier now serves three airports in the Lone Star State: Dallas/Fort Worth, McKinney, and Houston Intercontinental.

McKinney is near Dallas, Concord is near Charlotte, and Lakeland is between Orlando and Tampa

Initially, Avelo had one aircraft based at McKinney. However, that'll jump to two this month and, eventually, three. When a trio of aircraft are stationed there, nearly a third of the budget carrier's fleet will be there, which perhaps illustrates the challenges that it has faced.

Simple Flying's Live Flight Tracker shows that the N801XT and N803XT, both of which are 737-800s, will be there this week, although the specific airframes may change in the future. Generally, each aircraft has four legs per day. For example, on November 19, the 20.8-year-old N803XT, which is ex-Turkish Airlines, will fly McKinney-Orlando-McKinney-Las Vegas-McKinney.

As part of the announcement, Allegiant will pull out of Chicago Midway. Find out more now!

They will all be from McKinney and, like the five mentioned earlier, will be brand-new markets. Significantly, it'll be the first time that Avelo has flown to Denver, albeit only with one route and four weekly departures. It'll mark Avelo's return to Colorado after last flying there in September 2024 from Burbank to Colorado Springs.

Previously, the airline also flew from Burbank to Grand Junction and from Burbank and Las Vegas to Fort Collins. The new route contributes to Avelo having a record number of flights to Atlanta, the world's busiest airport for passengers but not for flights

However, context is needed. Only three markets will exist: Lakeland, McKinney, and Tweed New Haven. Previously, Avelo flew there from Wilmington, Delaware. Things are not this simple, as it appears that Lakeland-Atlanta service will end in early January 2027. No flights are scheduled beyond there, but that might change.

US Department of Transportation (DOT) information currently extends to August 2026. In the 12 months to that summer month, Avelo transported nearly 2.3 million round-trip passengers. Compared to the prior 12 months, traffic fell by 15%. While not a like-for-like comparison, Breeze grew by 47%.

Avelo's average load factor is 77.7%. While that is low compared to many budget operators around the world, it is relatively normal for US airlines. As the number of seats for sale fell by 16%, the smaller reduction in traffic (-15%) means that the load factor improved to the highest level since the 12 months to August 2023. What is not known is how fares changed.

Ares Capital Turned $10,000 Into Nearly $31,000 Over 10 Years. Can Its Dividend Do It Again?

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The leading BDC is still a reliable income investment for compounding total returns.

10 stocks we like better than Ares Capital ›

Ares Capital (ARCC -0.13%), the largest publicly traded business development company (BDC), pays a massive forward dividend yield of 10.2%. That high yield might seem like a red flag, but it's actually maintained stable or increasing dividends for the past 17 years.

Over the past 10 years, Ares' stock price only rose about 20%. But with reinvested dividends, it delivered a total return of 209% -- which would have turned a $10,000 investment into about $30,900. Should investors count on Ares to replicate those gains over the next ten years?

BDCs provide debt and equity financing to smaller "middle market" businesses that struggle to secure loans from conventional banks because they're considered riskier borrowers. In exchange for taking on more risk, BDCs charge higher interest rates than banks.

Ares has invested in 636 companies across its $29.2 billion portfolio. To reduce its overall risk, it allocates 58% of its portfolio to first-lien secured loans and 4% to second-lien secured loans.

To grow at a steady rate, Ares needs the Fed to keep interest rates in a "Goldilocks" zone between 3% and 4%. Lower rates will help its portfolio companies but reduce its net interest income, while higher rates will hurt its portfolio companies but boost its net interest income.

The Fed funds rate, at 3.75%-4.00%, is currently in that ideal range. But if inflation stays hot, another rate hike could squeeze its portfolio companies.

As a BDC, Ares continually issues new shares to finance its loans and investments. However, it also needs to pay at least 90% of its taxable income as dividends to maintain a lower tax rate. Therefore, most of the profits from Ares' investments flow directly into its massive dividends.

BDCs usually fund their dividends with their net interest income (NII) per share rather than their earnings per share (EPS). Its net asset value (NAV) per share also gives us a clearer view of how its investments are faring, and whether its stock is overvalued relative to those assets.

From 2015 to 2025, Ares' NII per share grew from $1.62 to $2.02, its annual dividends rose from $1.57 to $1.92, and its NAV per share increased from $16.46 to $19.94. At $19, it trades at a discount to its most recent NAV per share of $19.31 in the third quarter of 2026.

Ares' core growth rates seem modest, but they're enough to fund its massive dividends. As long as its NII per share covers its dividends and its stock doesn't become overvalued, it could replicate its impressive gains from the past decade through reinvested, compounded dividends.

Europe’s Inland Barges Need a Cargo Plan Before an Electric Fleet Plan

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Coal throughput at Rotterdam rose 15.3% in the first nine months of 2026. Crude oil and petroleum-product volumes also increased. An inland vessel operator looking only at this year’s cargo market could see decades of work ahead. But an engine, battery conversion or replacement vessel bought now may still be operating in 2050, when the mix of cargo travelling through European ports and rivers will be different.

Rotterdam’s latest throughput release explains the coal rebound through recovering coking-coal movements, expensive natural gas and power-market conditions. Meanwhile, Eurostat reports that EU inland-waterway freight fell 3% in 2025 to 118 billion tonne-kilometres. Coal and crude-petroleum freight rose that year, while agricultural freight contracted. These changes are reshaping the services that ports, vessel owners and customers need to buy.

The Central Commission for the Navigation of the Rhine counted 6,224 dry-cargo vessels in Rhine countries in 2025, down from 6,410 in 2023. Smaller vessels account for much of the decline. Tankers moved the other way, rising from 1,400 in 2022 to 1,477 in 2025. The numbers describe a fleet already adapting to changing cargo and operating economics.

My TFIE Strategy Briefing on the inland barges Europe will need in 2050 uses a cargo-first model to work from the freight task to the future electric fleet. The underlying 98-page Inland Freight Electrification report is available to read.

A vessel owner has two connected capital decisions. The first is whether the freight service will still have customers through the useful life of the asset. The second is which propulsion and charging system can perform that service economically. Starting with equipment and then finding a market for it risks spending scarce investment capital on the wrong vessels.

I modelled a large European industrial port corridor from a 2025-style baseline to 2050. The central scenario assumes bulk cargo falls from 57.8 million tonnes to 34.4 million tonnes, a decline of about 40%. Wet bulk accounts for much of the loss, falling from 34.5 million to 15.0 million tonnes. Dry bulk declines from 23.3 million to 19.4 million tonnes. The model is a transparent scenario for investment decisions across this type of corridor, rather than a forecast of Rotterdam’s exact future cargo volumes.

The largest change is fossil-linked cargo. Fossil fuels and refinery streams fall from 19.0 million tonnes to 1.5 million tonnes, while coal and other solid fossil fuels fall from 2.5 million tonnes to zero. Combined, those reductions account for about 20 million tonnes of the scenario’s 23.4-million-tonne net decline. Any operator whose principal customers depend on moving coal or petroleum faces a different replacement decision from one carrying grain, construction minerals, chemicals or scrap.

Steel illustrates what cargo substitution means. The model reduces imported iron ore and other virgin iron units from 4.5 million tonnes to 3.0 million tonnes, while scrap and circular feedstocks rise from 1.8 million to 3.5 million tonnes. The industrial function continues as the materials moving to and from factories change. Existing barges and terminals may find new work, although parcel sizes, loading systems and routes will also change.

Biofuel feedstocks create a second trap. In the central scenario those movements peak near 5 million tonnes in 2030 before falling to 1.5 million tonnes in 2050. A temporary freight boom can support profitable operations without supporting a new vessel or terminal investment with a thirty-year life. The durability of the contract matters as much as the volume carried this year.

Cargo tonnage alone cannot size an electric fleet. The model translates commodities into powered vessel-equivalents using parcel sizes, routes, loaded calls and productivity. It starts with around 1,150 powered vessel-equivalents and ends with 664, a reduction of 42%. Dry cargo falls 17% in tonnes but 43% in powered vessel-equivalents because the modelled freight service changes. The starting 1,150 powered equivalents represent about 1,234 physical hulls after counting unpowered barges moved by pushers. Battery conversion decisions concern powered units; terminal and cargo-handling decisions also concern the additional hulls.

That distinction is particularly important for pushed formations. A pusher provides propulsion for several cargo barges. Converting one powered unit does not necessarily mean replacing every physical hull. Counting all hulls as propulsion conversions would inflate the equipment and financing requirement.

Low Rhine water levels bring a different constraint into the same calculation. A barge can remain on the register yet carry less freight per journey when draught is restricted. Cargo owners may have to buy more voyages, use different hulls or shift some freight to rail and road. Route depth, seasonal load factors and terminal access belong in the service model alongside cargo demand.

Ports should begin with a living cargo and route register showing which freight is declining, which remains, and which changes its material inputs. Vessel owners can connect that register to remaining hull life, annual utilization, cargo contracts and the cost of electric conversion. Utilities and terminal operators can then size grid connections, charging facilities and battery-container pools against vessels that will run on bankable routes. Infrastructure developed around dependable freight customers has a stronger prospect of earning a return.

Europe has good reasons to electrify established, high-utilization inland freight now. Battery-powered vessels are already serving container and bulk routes. The commercial opportunity is to invest in routes with surviving customers and electrify the services they will continue to buy. Expanding an electric fleet before measuring that freight task puts the order of decisions backward.

The complete TFIE Strategy Briefing works through the cargo substitutions, fleet denominator and investment decisions. The open Inland Freight Electrification report includes route-energy modelling, battery-pool sizing, terminal requirements and programme economics. TFIE Strategy also undertakes scoped corridor and fleet assessments for ports, operators and cargo owners.

The early reviews for the Madden movie aren't good

📅 2026-11-18  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Madden movie, starring Nicolas Cage as the legendary coach, broadcaster, and video-game mogul, has landed on Prime Video. In the eyes of critics, it has landed with a thud.

The current consensus at Rotten Tomatoes, based on 16 reviews, is 13 percent.

The folks at Sports Business Journal have collected and summarized some of the feedback. Owen Gleiberman of Variety calls it a “stunt biopic on cinematic amphetamines,” adding that “it’s like an SNL sketch shot and edited by Scorsese.”

Then there’s this incredible lede from David Rooney of the Hollywood Reporter: “Imagine being cornered at a party while sober by a wired cokehead determined to tell you a long, painstakingly detailed story without pausing for breath and you might have some idea of the exhausting experience of watching Madden.”

Bryan Curtis of The Ringer says "[i]t’s not quite as awful as the trailers made it look,” but that it still “isn’t good.” Curtis also says Christian Bale is “mesmerizing” as Al Davis, which raises the question of why they didn’t just make an Al Davis movie?

Of course, I’ll still watch it. You should, too. But I have a feeling that, throughout the experience, I’ll be wondering how much John Madden himself would have hated it.

LO3 Capital Announces Investment in Regiment Security Partners

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

YONKERS, N.Y., Nov. 18, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- LO3 Capital ("LO3") is pleased to announce a new platform investment in Regiment Security Partners ("RSP" or the "Company"). LO3 partnered with the existing management team at RSP, along with Expedition Capital Partners, Capital Alignment Partners, and Tecum Capital Partners on the transaction and will be focused on continued growth, both organic and acquired, going forward.

Formed in 2019 with roots dating back to 1980, RSP is headquartered in Yonkers, NY, and provides contracted security services to a diverse base of blue-chip customers across the United States. The Company offers manned security guarding, mobile patrol, special event, executive protection and consulting services to retail, commercial, institutional, government, residential, healthcare, grocery and industrial customers. The investment will support RSP's continued organic growth, investment in operating leadership and business development, expansion of its national partner program and strategic acquisitions that strengthen the Company's position as a premier national security services provider.

"This partnership is about building on what has made RSP successful: well-trained officers, responsive service, and customers who trust us to protect their people and property," commented Dan Quinn, Chief Executive Officer of Regiment Security Partners. "Our partners share our vision for the business, and their support positions us to invest in our team, expand our partner network and pursue acquisitions that broaden the value we deliver to our customers and communities."

"Dan and his team have built RSP into a national platform with a deeply loyal customer base," said Wesley Roberts, Partner and CFO of LO3 Capital. "We're proud to back them in the Company's next phase of growth."

Speaking on behalf of Expedition Capital Partners, Mike Hendrie, Founder and Managing Director, emphasized the Company's strong foundation and growth potential: "RSP has built a reputation for dependable, high-quality service. We're excited to work alongside Dan and the team to accelerate organic growth, pursue strategic acquisitions and grow the platform into a leading national security services company."

This transaction marks LO3 Capital's eighteenth investment since its September 2022 launch.

LO3 Capital is a minority-owned private investment firm committed to helping lower-middle market companies grow. The firm has offices in Nashville, New York, and Dallas and targets businesses with over $3 million of EBITDA across a variety of industries. LO3 Capital partners with management teams, business owners, and private equity firms to finance organic growth, acquisitions, recapitalizations, and ownership transitions. LO3 Capital was previously a division of Live Oak Growth Capital and continues to manage the Live Oak Growth Capital funds.

For more information, please visit www.lo3capital.com.

GLP-1 Eye Warning Leaves Patients, Doctors With a Difficult Choice

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

— FDA should study outcomes when patients stop, continue, or change treatment after NAION

When someone loses vision in one eye while taking a GLP-1 drug, the first priority is an urgent eye examination. If the diagnosis is non-arteritic anterior ischemic optic neuropathy (NAION), the question that follows is: should we stop the drug?

On November 9, FDA announced it is requiring new warnings across all products in the GLP-1 drug class, which includes semaglutide (Ozempic, Wegovy) and tirzepatide (Zepbound, Mounjaro), among others, about a possible increased risk of NAION.

NAION occurs when inadequate blood flow damages the nerve that carries visual information from the eye to the brain. It can cause sudden, painless vision loss, often noticed on waking. The loss can be permanent, and there is no proven treatment to reverse it. The condition is rare, but these medications are widely used: in a March 2026 KFF poll, about one in eight U.S. adults reported currently taking a GLP-1 drug and one in five reported ever taking one.

As an ophthalmologist, I have routinely asked patients with suspected NAION about GLP-1 use for the past 2 years. I welcome the warning. The FDA should now pair it with a plan to study what happens when patients stop, continue, or change treatment after a NAION diagnosis.

People who develop NAION in one eye are already at increased risk of developing it in the other. How GLP-1 drugs might contribute remains uncertain. One hypothesis is that rapid reductions in blood sugar could cause swelling where the optic nerve enters the eye, compromising blood flow in susceptible patients. That possibility does not tell us whether continuing the medication adds to the risk in the other eye, or whether stopping reduces it.

These medications can be difficult to give up. Some patients take them to manage diabetes that has resisted other treatment. Certain drugs in the class also reduce the risk of heart attacks or worsening kidney disease in eligible patients. Stopping requires a plan for the condition being treated. When counseling patients who develop NAION while taking a GLP-1 drug, I recommend involving the prescribing doctor before changing treatment.

Regulators around the world have responded differently to this dilemma. European guidance says to stop semaglutide if NAION is confirmed. The FDA, whose warning covers the broader drug class, advises clinicians to consider discontinuation unless the benefits clearly outweigh the risk. I support that approach, with the patient, ophthalmologist, and prescribing doctor weighing the potential harm to vision against the benefits of treatment with a GLP-1.

Across 126 clinical trials, the FDA identified 15 probable NAION cases. The rates were equivalent to roughly six cases per 100,000 patients followed for a year among those receiving GLP-1 drugs, compared with four among those receiving a placebo or another diabetes medication. Larger observational studies have also raised concern, though their estimates vary and they cannot fully separate the effects of treatment from the conditions that led patients to receive it.

But estimating the risk of developing NAION does not tell us whether a patient should continue a GLP-1 drug after NAION diagnosis. One major FDA study excluded people with previous damage to the optic nerve from inadequate blood flow to help identify new cases accurately. That was a sensible design for its purpose, but it also means the study cannot establish whether continuing treatment after a diagnosis changes the risk to the other eye.

Some research has examined second-eye involvement. An analysis presented at the American Academy of Ophthalmology annual meeting this fall found that, among patients who developed NAION in both eyes, the second eye was affected sooner in GLP-1 users. It did not establish whether stopping would have prevented that outcome. Nor do we have reliable comparative evidence that switching to another GLP-1 drug is safer.

The FDA should assess whether its existing safety surveillance can answer these questions. If those systems fall short, the agency should require manufacturers to support additional studies. Federal law gives the agency that authority for serious safety concerns when routine reporting and available surveillance are insufficient.

The work could begin in participating eye clinics, linking confirmed diagnoses to prescribing and medical records. Researchers would document which medication a patient was taking, whether it was stopped or changed, when, and why. They would follow vision in the other eye alongside overall health, including how well replacement treatment managed diabetes or other conditions. Researchers would need to account for differences in diabetes severity and visual damage that affect both treatment decisions and outcomes.

The FDA should publish the study plans, identify who is responsible, and set dates for progress updates. Funding should be disclosed and results analyzed independently of manufacturers. Because NAION is uncommon, firm answers may take years. Patients and clinicians should still be able to see what is being learned and what remains uncertain.

The patient deciding what to do about the next dose cannot wait years for better evidence. Today's decision will have to be made with what we know. We owe that patient our best judgment, and we should follow what happens afterward. The next person facing the same choice deserves more to go on.

Your Bill Due Dates Aren’t Fixed – Move Them to Follow Your Paychecks

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

When your bills come due before the paycheck that covers them, ask to move the due dates. Most card issuers let you pick a new date online, and many lenders and utilities will do the same. Set each bill to land after the payday that covers it, and the late fees and overdrafts stop.

Say you’re paid every other Friday, and rent, the car payment, and a card bill all come due between the 1st and the 5th. One paycheck often has to carry all three, so a pricey week before the 1st can end in an overdraft or a late payment.

Pay that comes every two weeks follows a pattern you can plan around. Any 14 days in a row hold exactly one payday, so one check always lands between the 1st and the 14th and another between the 15th and the 28th.

List every bill with its due date and amount, then split them between two dates. Bills due early in the month draw on the check that landed between the 15th and the 28th of the month before. Bills due around the 17th draw on the one that lands by the 14th, at least three days ahead. Leave rent where your lease puts it and build the early group around it. In the example, the car payment and card bill move to the 17th, and each paycheck now carries its own share. If you’re paid on the 15th and the last day of the month, put the mid-month bills on the 18th, three days after that paycheck.

Start with your cards. They’re the easiest bills to move, and a card payment that reaches 30 days late generally goes on your credit report. Expect a billing cycle or two before the new date kicks in, and keep paying by the old one until a statement shows the change.

Car loans allow fewer moves than cards. Chase lets you change an auto loan’s due date twice over the life of the loan, and Wells Fargo just once. Many utilities run a preferred due date program, and some cap changes at one a year. Pick dates you can keep, then set each bill’s autopay for its new due date.

It’s an afternoon of clicks and calls, so do it this week. Then build a month of cushion in checking. Once it’s there, due dates stop mattering at all.
